All unreachable. `RecordHospitalReturnDialog` closes it; verified end-to-end through PostgREST as an authenticated `org_admin` (episode → `returned`, work item `hospital_return`/open/24h, review seeded `draft`, timeline row) |
| G2 | Citation verification has no write path | M | done | Phase 10b shipped `record_citation_verification`, `record_citation_superseded` and `get_citation_governance_status`, platform-admin only, and **nothing calls any of them**. `citationGovernance.ts` is wired to *display* the qualifier, so the readiness table correctly shows citations as approximate/unverified — but there is no surface through which anyone can ever record a verification. The plan's own position is that seeding needs a compliance SME with the regulation in front of them; this row is the narrower point that even an SME has no way in. `CitationGovernanceSection` (lazy, 9.1 KiB, mounted on `/admin/regulatory-updates`) is the way in: status counts, a stale-verification warning, the full topic table with each row's live qualifier, and dialogs over both write RPCs. `verificationFormIssues` states the server's rules up front rather than letting the operator find out by submitting — the server still enforces them, and both refusals were exercised. Verified end-to-end over PostgREST as a platform admin: a sourceless verification is refused ("Verification requires the source the citation was read from"), a real one moves the row to `verified` with the verifier and date stamped, the governance summary went `{unverified:16, approximate:6}` -> `{verified:1, unverified:16, approximate:5}`, and a supersession with no successor is refused. **No content was seeded** — that still needs a compliance SME with the regulation open, which is the plan's position and unchanged |
| G3 | Admissions pipeline stage has no advance path | S | done | `advance_admission_pipeline_stage` has no caller, so the Phase 9a funnel is read-only through the UI. **Checked, and it was worse than the row guessed.** `update_admission_prospect` writes `stage` (the decision lifecycle); `pipeline_stage` (the fourteen-stage funnel) is written by `advance_admission_pipeline_stage` and by nothing else, so after `20260726170000`'s one-time backfill every new prospect sat at its `new_inquiry` default permanently — the funnel, its conversion rates, its referral-source ROI and its weighted pipeline value were all computed over a column that never moved. **What hid it:** the review dialog's one stage control was *labelled* "Pipeline stage" and bound to `stage`, so it looked like the funnel had a write path. That label now says "Decision stage", and a real funnel control sits beside it, saved by its own RPC and showing the stage's meaning plus a note when the move is backwards. Backwards is permitted deliberately — `admissionPipeline.ts` says a funnel that refuses to record a regression gets worked around in a spreadsheet. Verified over PostgREST as an `org_admin`: `new_inquiry` -> `tour_scheduled` accepted, an unknown stage refused, a backwards move to `contact_attempted` accepted |
| G4 | Audit the other verified-dead RPCs | S | done | **Audited; each one checked rather than assumed, which changed the answer three times.** Results split into G5–G8 below. Two turned out not to be dead ends at all: `get_resident_administrative_packet` is a superseded read (the record reads the table directly and writes through `save_resident_administrative_master`), and `grant_duty_eligibility_override` is a *second* override path alongside the wired `create_schedule_eligibility_override` — a duplicate-concept question, not a gap, and the same shape as the two-ledger and digest/sweep divergences this repo has closed before |
| G5 | Medication resident mapping had no write path | M | done | `map_medication_resident` is the only writer of `medication_resident_mappings` and had no caller. An `unmatched_resident` exception — the eMAR sending administration data for somebody CareBase cannot identify — could be acknowledged, assigned an owner, given an SLA work item and dismissed, but **never actually fixed**, because fixing it means creating the mapping. Everything downstream joins through that row, so the administration evidence for an unmapped resident never reaches their chart or timeline. The exception dialog now offers "Map to resident" for exactly that exception type; the RPC resolves the exception itself, so the two cannot disagree. Verified over PostgREST as an `org_admin`: mapping row created, exception auto-resolved with "Resident mapping completed" |
| G6 | A completed training class cannot be corrected | S | done | **Confirmed by the database itself:** seeding a completed class's attendance directly raises *"completed class attendance is immutable; use the audited correction RPC"* — the RPC that had no caller. `complete_training_class` was wired; `correct_completed_training_class` and `correct_completed_class_attendee` were not, by anything, so the one legitimate way to fix a wrong attendance record did not exist in the product. `CompletedClassCorrectionCard` (lazy, org_admin/platform_admin only, matching `assert_completed_class_corrector`) is that surface. It offers only the four fields the server accepts, because hours and dates are what each attendee's training record was computed from. `completedClassCorrection.ts` pins the 10-character reason minimum and the field allow-list to the migration SQL by test, so the form and the server cannot drift. Verified over PostgREST as an `org_admin`: short reason refused, `scheduled_hours` refused, a real correction applied, and correcting attendance absent→attended **created the training record**, which is what compliance is computed from |
| G7 | A shift swap request cannot be withdrawn | S | done | `request_shift_swap` and `decide_shift_swap` were both wired; `cancel_shift_swap_request` was not, so a requester could raise a swap but not withdraw one they no longer wanted — it sat in the manager's queue until decided, and the only way out was asking a manager to deny something nobody was asking for any more. A pending-swaps card on `MySchedule` with a Withdraw action, deliberately self-service rather than a second manager control: the RPC scopes itself to `e.profile_id = auth.uid()`, so only the requester can use it, and `shift_swap_requests_select` already let an employee read their own rows so no new policy was needed. Verified over PostgREST: a short reason refused, the withdrawal applied with its reason stored, and withdrawing twice refused (*"Only pending swaps can be canceled"*) |
| G8 | Certification attempts had no entry point at all | L | done | **The row was wrong about the shape, and checking is what found it.** It read as "approval has no surface". The truth: `certification_attempts` and `certification_attempt_items` have full structure and **nothing anywhere creates a row in either** — no RPC, no edge function, no trigger. So `approve_certification_attempt`, which is rigorous (assessor qualified *at observation time*, separation of duties, checklist published and effective then, every required item evidenced and signed, signed decision plus evidence checksum), approved rows that could not exist, and `in_progress` / `submitted` were unreachable states. Building only the approval screen would have produced a screen over a permanently-empty table, so the scope grew deliberately: `20260804120000` adds `start_certification_attempt`, `record_certification_attempt_item` and `submit_certification_attempt` — the observation path — and `CertificationAttemptSection` (lazy, on the employee record's Credentials tab, org-scoped because `assert_phase3_admin` admits org_admin and `qualifications.manage` holders, not only platform admins). `app_private.certification_attempt_blockers` checks the three preconditions knowable *before* an observation, so an assessor is refused before spending an hour at a bedside; it is deliberately a **subset**, not a second gate — approve re-checks everything and stays authoritative. 18 pgTAP assertions drive the whole path as an authenticated `org_admin`, ending on the two that matter: the approval function runs for the first time, and the employee holds the `active` qualification it exists to grant |
| G9 | Survey packet guest access was a one-way door | M | done | **Found by measuring what the first sweep asserted.** That pass said 79 uncalled RPCs were "most legitimately" so and verified ten by hand; re-running it against *all* SQL — other function bodies, triggers, policies, cron — turned the assertion into a count: of 69 with no client or edge caller, **49 are genuinely live server-side, 12 are exercised only by pgTAP, and 8 are reachable by nothing at all.** The sharpest of the 8: `issue_survey_packet_guest_grant` is wired and `revoke_survey_packet_guest_grant` is not, and **no surface listed existing grants**. So a survey evidence packet — compliance evidence assembled for an external surveyor — could be shared with a guest, and afterwards nobody could see who held access or take it back. The same product already does this correctly for move-in guests, where issue *and* revoke are both wired. `GuestGrantList` now shows every grant with its state, expiry and download count, keeping revoked and expired ones visible because who *used to* have access is the auditor's question, and offers Revoke with a required reason. One real correctness detail found while verifying: the RPC returns `false` rather than raising when the grant is missing, so the hook treats a non-`true` result as a failure — resolving successfully there would tell somebody access was withdrawn when nothing was. Verified over PostgREST as an `org_admin`: short reason refused, missing grant returns false, real revocation stamps `revoked_at`/`revoked_by`/`revocation_reason` |
| G10 | The rest of the measured sweep | L | done | **Nineteen findings, and the measurement was right about most of them and wrong about one in a way worth writing down.** Eight fixes, each verified by driving the RPC over PostgREST as a real authenticated user rather than by reading the SQL. **`bulk_update_alert_status`:** `useBulkUpdateAlerts` was updating the `alerts` table directly, so bulk resolution skipped the `alerts_bulk_status_updated` audit log, never stamped `resolved_at` (making a human-resolved alert indistinguishable from a nightly-sweep one) and bypassed per-alert facility authorization; it now calls the RPC and surfaces per-row `failed`/`unauthorized` outcomes instead of reporting success for rows the server refused. **`revoke_class_checkin_tokens`:** generate was wired and rotating every 30s while revoke had no caller — rotation is not revocation, so a code that left the room kept working until it rotated; `QrCheckinCard` now revokes with a required reason. **Governed content — the largest, and it needed a migration:** `GovernedLearning` dispatched only `review_governed_content_revision`, against a revision ID typed into a text box, so it reviewed revisions nothing could create and an approved one could never be published — 3 of 4 lifecycle steps unreachable. Wiring the three RPCs was not enough: `governed_content_assets`, the row every revision hangs off, has `grant select` and nothing else — no INSERT grant, no RLS write policy, no RPC — so in production the table is empty for every tenant with no way to add a row, and `create_governed_content_revision` would have raised 'Governed asset not found' for every course. `20260804130000` adds `register_governed_content_asset` (idempotent on the existing unique key, because bringing a course under governance is a statement about the course rather than an event), and `GovernedContentRevisionsPanel` walks author → submit → review → publish with separation of duties stated up front rather than surfaced as a 42501. Snapshots are built from the course version's own blocks and sorted, so re-snapshotting an unchanged version hashes identically and a reviewer can tell nothing moved. 15 pgTAP assertions drive registration and the whole lifecycle as two authenticated users. **`record_resident_dme_event`:** the register card promised it 'preserves assignment history and repair/inspection documentation' and nothing could write a history row after registration; worse, the 'DME inspections due' metric counts in-use items with no `inspected` row inside their frequency window, so it could only ever rise — every item with an inspection frequency was permanently overdue from the day it was registered. Proven on a live stack: the count went 1 → 0 after recording an inspection through the new register. **`end_enterprise_role_grant`:** the console could issue an effective-dated grant and never close one, a one-way door on standing access; `StandingGrantsCard` lists open grants with their age and ends them with a required reason (verified: blank reason 22023, end-before-start 22007, valid call closes the grant and appends the reason to its permanent record). **`create_hris_import_run` + `upsert_enterprise_role_template`:** Validate and Apply were both wired to a run ID typed by hand, and nothing minted one, so neither was reachable in practice; the grant console likewise asked for a role template as a raw UUID and a tenant could never define one beyond the six seeded built-ins. Both now have real pickers and a create path (verified: same request ID returns the same run, unknown permission and empty permission set both refused). **`get_notification_delivery_health` / `preview_notification_template`:** the console could retry one failed delivery but could not see whether the queue was moving at all — a stuck worker looked like a quiet day — and could only preview the draft in the editor, never the saved version that is actually sending. **The correction:** this row previously guessed `record_service_exception_follow_up` 'may be a superseded duplicate' because the documentation path persists follow-up answers. Checking it: `record_service_task_response` does set `supervisor_notified`, but that is a self-report by the person documenting, and **only** the dormant function creates the `service-exception:<id>` work item — a tracked row with a due date someone must close. So the gap was real but narrower than 'no route to a supervisor', and the button is deliberately offered even when the box was ticked. **Ruled out rather than built:** `set_package_entitlement` is not a dead end — an `ingest_legacy_package_contract` trigger already syncs `packages.features` into `package_entitlements`, which the resolver reads, so the live path works and the RPC only adds effective-dating and a contract reference that no requirement asks for yet. **Recorded, not built, for want of a host surface:** `plan_audit_archive`, `simulate_workflow_automation_rule`, `set_facility_clinical_enabled`, `evaluate_learning_path` (driven by the runtime, not an admin), `create_deduplicated_work_item`; and `unassign_organization_release_cohort`, whose assign counterpart is itself unwired. The sweep now reports **4 reachable by nothing and 6 pgTAP-only, down from 8 and 12** |
| G11 | The sweep reaches zero, and running it found a bug the sweep could not see | L | done | **The measurement was wrong in one direction and the running was right in another.** First the correction: the sweep read `create or replace` and `grant execute` out of migration text and never looked for `drop function`, so it reported `unassign_organization_release_cohort` as a finding six days after `20260802030000_remove_pilot_program.sql` deliberately removed it. Checking the live database rather than the files settled it in one query. The script now honours drops, and 4 of its previous "findings" were functions that do not exist. **Six real ones fixed.** `plan_audit_archive` was the only writer of `audit_archive_batches`, so the deliberate "freeze this range, hash it, record whether a legal hold covers it" step had no beginning; the Data Lifecycle panel now previews the manifest with the same function the plan stores, and the planned batch came back with the identical row count and digest. `simulate_workflow_automation_rule` had no caller while `run_workflow_automation_now` did — so the only way to learn what a rule would do was to let it do it, creating real work items and notifying real people; Value Center now dry-runs, and the simulation is most useful on a draft, which "Run now" refuses. `set_facility_clinical_enabled` is the only writer of a column that gates native clinical charting, so it could only ever hold its default `true` and a facility switched off by a direct write could never be switched back on. `set_package_entitlement` was **not** the dead end this register guessed either — a trigger already syncs `packages.features` into the table the resolver reads — but it is the only way to express a term with dates and a contract reference, and the probe shows exactly that: the immediate `legacy_backfill` row closed at the term boundary and a `sales_contract` term took over from 1 September. `create_deduplicated_work_item` was the sharpest: **the work queue could only ever fill itself**, every item arriving from a detected condition, with no create path in the client at all — and it is the only creator that goes through a template, which is what makes closure evidence enforceable. **And one that needed a migration, because there was nothing to call it on.** `evaluate_learning_path` is careful work — pinned definitions, prerequisites, score thresholds selecting a remedial branch, an explainable transition event per step — and **nothing anywhere wrote `learning_path_definitions`, `learning_path_versions` or `learning_path_assignments`**. No RPC, no trigger, no seed. `20260804140000` supplies authoring, publication and assignment, refusing the three shapes that make a path unwalkable (a prerequisite naming a step that is not in the path does not error at evaluation — it locks that step forever with an explanation that cannot say why). 22 pgTAP assertions walk the whole thing. **The bug reading could not have found.** Driving the new adaptive surface, the stale-state-version case never returned — not a 4xx, not a 5xx, a hung request and several concurrently active backends from one client call. Isolated with a throwaway function that does nothing but raise: `55000` answered instantly, `22023` answered instantly, **`40001` never returned**. PostgREST treats SQLSTATE 40001 as the transient serialization failure it is named for and retries; these are *deterministic* precondition checks that will never pass on retry. Four functions did this, and **two are already wired to live UI** — so today a manager deciding a shift swap somebody else changed underneath them gets a request that never comes back instead of "shift assignments changed after swap request". `20260804150000` redeclares all four with `55000`, the blocks copied programmatically rather than retyped so the only difference is five characters. **And one asymmetry the removal left behind.** That same pilot-program removal kept `assign_organization_release_cohort` and dropped its counterpart, while stating "the general release-flag / cohort / kill-switch mechanism itself is untouched". It was touched: an organization could be put into a cohort and nothing supported could take it out. `20260804160000` restores the original unassign verbatim — completing the removal's own stated intent rather than re-adding the console it deliberately deleted. Verified: assign creates the membership, unassign removes it. **The sweep now reports 0 and 0** — every function granted to `authenticated` has a client caller, an edge caller, or a live server-side reference |
| G12 | The gate that reported zero was counting a comment as a caller | M | done | **The zero this branch reported was not true, and the thing making it look true was the gate added to guarantee it.** A review flagged that `check-dormant-rpcs.mjs` collected every `drop function` across the whole scan and subtracted at the end, so a function dropped once and re-created later stayed excluded -- `unassign_organization_release_cohort` is dropped by `20260802030000` and restored by `20260804160000`, and the end-of-scan subtraction removed it from the check even though it is granted and live. That was real. Fixing it exposed a worse one the review had not seen: with the drop handled, the gate still passed, because `isCallSite` stripped declarative statements but not **comments**. The line excusing the function was the migration's own header explaining why it had been removed. Naming a function in prose -- which is what a good comment does -- marked it reachable. Both are fixed: drops apply per migration in filename order, and comments are stripped from the whole file (block comments before the line split, since a line-at-a-time reader cannot see it is inside one) before any line is tested. Self-test grew from 9 cases to 14, covering cross-migration drop-then-restore, a drop never undone, and prose naming a function. **The sweep now reports six, not zero.** One restored by this branch, five pre-existing on `main` and never caught because the hand-run sweep shared the same blind spot. Each is verified against the live catalog with a word-boundary check -- `LIKE '%name%'` first reported two callers for `get_resident_administrative_packet` that turned out to be its own renamed predecessors -- and each is allowlisted with a specific reason and an open row below. An entry in that allowlist is a debt, not an absolution |
| G12.1 | `unassign_organization_release_cohort` has no caller | S | done | **Wired, together with its counterpart.** The statement-level gate fix showed `assign_organization_release_cohort` is equally uncalled, so this was never a half-wired mechanism -- it was an entirely unreachable one, with the tables intact. `ReleaseCohortMembershipCard` lives on the release-flags page beside the flags and kill switches, which is what `20260802030000` said it was keeping. Deliberately not a re-creation of the pilot console it deleted: no pilot programme, no cohort authoring, just membership of cohorts that already exist. Previously: Restored by `20260804160000` to close the one-way door `20260802030000` left: assign was kept, unassign dropped, so an organization could be put into a release cohort and nothing supported could take it out. The function is the supported exit; the console that called it was deleted on purpose and is deliberately not re-added here. Needs a surface |
| G12.2 | `admin_emergency_update_course_block` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** `EmergencyBlockCorrection`, offered per block on a published (locked) version and only to a platform admin -- the two conditions the server already enforced. The lock stays the rule; this is the recorded exception, with the reason and the before/after in `audit_logs`. Previously: Pre-existing on `main`. An emergency edit path for a published course block, with its own audit trail, that no admin screen offers |
| G12.3 | `get_resident_administrative_packet` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** `AdministrativePacketCard` on the resident Documents tab. Shown as which of the nine sections are populated rather than as a rendered file, because every section already has a screen that owns it -- what nothing answered was "is anything missing before I hand this over", and an empty section is the finding a file review is looking for. Downloads the packet as it came back. Previously: Pre-existing on `main`. The current wrapper in a chain of renamed predecessors (`_base`, `_before_dietary`, `_before_calendar`); every rename carried the grant forward and nothing reads the packet it assembles |
| G12.4 | `grant_duty_eligibility_override` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** `DutyEligibilityCard` on the employee record. This also gave `src/lib/dutyEligibility.ts` its first consumer: that module had modelled `overrideId` and an `overridable` flag per reason from the start and was imported by nothing, so the override is offered only where the library says it is a sensible answer -- "this person has no account" is not something to wave through, and the button does not appear. Previously: Pre-existing on `main`. Duty eligibility can be evaluated and refused, but the documented override that lets a supervisor accept the risk has no way in |
| G12.5 | `is_employee_access_active` has no caller | S | done | **Wired, and it corrected a badge that was lying.** The employee header read `profile_id != null` and announced "Portal access active", which is also true of a suspended employee and of a deactivated account; `is_employee_access_active` weighs employment status, the linked account and any suspension window together. `employee_access_suspensions` had no surface anywhere in the product. The `p_at` argument is why this is a function rather than a column -- once a suspension is lifted, nothing else can answer "did they have access the day of the incident", so `EmployeeAccessCard` asks it directly. Previously: Pre-existing on `main`. Reads as a helper predicate, but no other function body, policy, trigger or view references it either -- dead rather than internal |
| G12.6 | `revoke_offline_service_device` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** `OfflineServiceDeviceCard` in Floor mode. Revoking is refused while unsynced drafts remain, and that refusal is the point rather than a nicety: the wipe is local and permanent, those drafts are care documentation that never reached the server, and nothing on the server can restore them. Previously: Pre-existing on `main`. Offline service devices can be registered but not revoked: the same one-way-door shape closed for survey-packet guest grants in G9 |
| G13 | The Codex review: twelve findings, twelve real | L | done | **Three P1s broke features this branch itself added.** Starting a certification attempt failed outright: the hook sent `p_observed_at: null` where the parameter has `default now()`, and a PostgreSQL default applies only when the argument is *omitted* -- an explicit null passes null into a NOT NULL column, so every start raised 23502. Proven against live PG rather than argued, and a sweep of all 278 functions with defaulted parameters confirmed it was the only instance (the other 471 defaulted parameters are `NULL::type`, where passing null changes nothing). A hospital return with new orders could never be closed -- the dialog writes only `pending_review`, the reconciliation refuses to close without `acknowledged`, and nothing wrote it; `20260804190000` adds `acknowledge_hospital_return_new_order` plus ack_at/_by/_note, so the record can say who accepted the orders and what changed. Annual medical evaluations were invisible to two edge functions: `generate-state-form-prefill` had no annual template key, so the State Forms Center answered "prefill only available" about a form sitting directly above it, and compliance-copilot still filtered a single item type, hiding every expired annual re-evaluation from the intent that exists to find them. **The nine P2s:** the funnel dropdown offered `admitted`, which the RPC refuses by design and `SETTABLE_STAGES` exists to exclude; Evaluate was offered on completed learning-path assignments, where an empty outcome would bump `state_version` and rewrite steps under a `completed` row; a closed appointment outcome with new orders still opened a follow-up nothing could close; a DME transfer parked the item in a `transferred` status, dropping it off the inspection schedule while it was still in service with a new resident; a completed-class correction invalidated a query key no hook uses; the archive Plan button was live while the row count was still loading, which is exactly how the empty batch it guards against gets made; the governed revision panel validated current course rows rather than the frozen snapshot the reviewer approves, wrong in both directions; and a checklist item recorded `not_applicable` -- which the record path accepts without evidence or signature -- could never be submitted or approved, because both completeness gates ignored `result`. That last one is covered by a new pgTAP suite that **fails against the pre-fix gate** ("These checklist items are not complete: insulin_draw") and passes against the fixed one. **The earlier review, for contrast:** two comments claiming Supabase throws plain objects so toasts would be blank. It does not here -- postgrest-js 2.110.1 declares `PostgrestError extends Error` -- but checking it found a narrower real path, since an RPC is a POST and POST is not retryable in postgrest-js, so a fetch failure rethrows the raw rejected value and bypasses the hook's `new Error(...)` wrap entirely. 8 non-defensive handlers, 6 of them pre-existing, now route through a shared `errorText` |
| G14 | The official form is cached, so CI stops asserting that pa.gov is up | M | done | **A test named for a PDF was really testing a government website.** `fetchDhsTemplate` did one unconditional `fetch` of `www.pa.gov` with a 15s abort, no cache and no retry, on the critical path of three edge functions -- so filling a reportable-incident form depended on DHS answering at that instant, and the E2E assertion "a reportable incident produces the official state-form PDF" failed twice on a slow pa.gov, with Playwright's single retry hitting the same wall. A suite that reports a product failure when a third party is slow is reporting the wrong thing. Now a read-through cache in a private `regulatory-templates` bucket, keyed by a hash of the source URL: DHS versions these documents in the URL itself (`...Effective-October-1-2016.pdf`), so a new form is a new key and a cached object never goes stale for the URL that produced it -- and there is deliberately no TTL, because a TTL would reintroduce the dependency on a schedule nobody chose. A miss retries three times with backoff and stores what it got; a form that genuinely cannot be downloaded still fails, because silently substituting another document for a regulated form would be worse. 8 Deno tests pin it, including that a cache hit does not touch the network at all, and the E2E `beforeAll` seeds the cache so the test measures what it is named after. **One thing this turned up:** `tenant_isolation_invariants` asserts every storage bucket is named by at least one object policy, and the new bucket needs none -- the edge functions use the service role. **Corrected after the fact:** the first attempt satisfied that invariant by granting platform admins a read nothing needed -- a capability invented to quiet a check. The invariant was corrected instead: every bucket is either policied or declared service-role-only in an explicit list with its reason, plus a second assertion that fails a stale declaration. Verified by breaking both |
| G15 | The gate read SQL a line at a time, and that hid twenty more | L | done | **Third correction to this script, and the largest.** A grant naming several functions puts one per line: only the first line carries `grant execute`, so every function after it read as a bare call and was excused from the check entirely. SQL is a statement language and this was reading it as lines. The check now drops whole grant/revoke/comment/drop/alter statements -- which can never contain a call -- while keeping `create function` BODIES, where most real calls live, and dropping only the signature so a function is not read as calling itself. Self-tests grew 14 -> 18, covering a multi-function grant, a multi-function revoke, a call inside a body surviving the header strip, and a function not counting as its own caller. **Twenty more dormant functions, every one verified against the live catalog rather than against this script's own parsing** -- word-boundary search over `pg_proc` bodies, RLS policies, views and triggers, plus client and edge source. The count has now been wrong three times, each time because the script read SQL badly, and each fix raised it: no `drop function` handling, then comments counted as callers, then this. "Zero" was never true. Twenty-one is the number. They are allowlisted with specific reasons and the rows below rather than wired here, because twenty capabilities is its own body of work and burying it in a 124-file pull request would hide it |
| G15.1 | `assign_organization_release_cohort` has no caller | S | done | **Wired with G12.1.** Its dormancy is the clearest example of what the line-by-line gate was hiding: it kept its grant through the pilot-console removal, lost its caller, and sat on a continuation line of a multi-function grant where nothing could see it. Previously: G12.1's counterpart, and dormant for the same reason: the pilot cohort console that called both was deleted on purpose by 20260802030000. Neither side has a caller; the mechanism is intact and unreachable. |
| G15.2 | `revoke_integration_api_credential` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** `IntegrationRegisterCard` on the enterprise control plane, nested inside the provisioning card so both read the same organization field: issue above, take back below. Previously: An issued integration API credential cannot be revoked from anywhere. The same one-way door closed for survey-packet guest grants in G9, on a credential that authenticates machine access. |
| G15.3 | `rotate_integration_api_credential` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** Rotation returns a plaintext key the server never stores again, so it is shown once and copied to the clipboard. Rotating needs no written reason -- rotating on a schedule is good practice and should not require an excuse -- while revoking does, because revoking answers an incident. Previously: Nor rotated, so a credential suspected of leaking can only be left in place. |
| G15.4 | `rotate_integration_webhook_secret` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** Same one-shot secret handling, with the warning that the consumer needs the new secret before the old one stops being accepted or deliveries start failing signature checks. Previously: The signing secret a webhook consumer verifies against cannot be rotated. |
| G15.5 | `deactivate_integration_webhook_endpoint` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** An endpoint can be switched off with a reason, and the register shows consecutive failures so a dead destination is visible before somebody goes looking. Previously: A webhook endpoint can be created but never switched off, so deliveries continue to a URL nobody controls any more. |
| G15.6 | `rotate_scim_connection_credential` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** `ScimRegistryCard` beside the create card. The registry shows a `credential_hint` rather than the credential, so the rotation panel says at the moment it matters that the secret is readable once. Previously: The SCIM provisioning credential cannot be rotated, which is the standing remediation when a directory integration is compromised. |
| G15.7 | `get_scim_connection_registry` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** Nothing could list a SCIM connection, which is why rotation had nowhere to be offered from. Previously: Takes no arguments and returns the SCIM connection registry. Nothing lists SCIM connections at all, so the rotation above has no surface to be offered from either. |
| G15.8 | `link_sso_identity_subject` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** For an identity the provider sends that matches no account -- usually because somebody's subject identifier changed -- which without this means the person cannot sign in and nothing in the product can attach them. Previously: Binds an SSO provider subject to a profile with a stated link method. Without it an SSO identity that fails to auto-match cannot be attached by an administrator. |
| G15.9 | `grant_identity_break_glass` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** `BreakGlassCard` on Security Governance, above Sensitive Activity because break-glass is what produces the activity below it. Requires a ticket reference and a reason, and caps the grant at 24 hours -- longer than that is not an emergency, it is a role. Previously: Emergency elevated access with a requester, a ticket reference, a reason and a mandatory expiry -- carefully built, and impossible to invoke. |
| G15.10 | `revoke_identity_break_glass` has no caller | S | done | **Wired, and this is the half that mattered.** A grant that cannot be revoked early runs to its expiry whatever the investigation finds. Previously: And impossible to end early, which is the half that matters most. |
| G15.11 | `record_training_attendance` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** `SessionRosterCard` on the class page. The signature is a hash of a name the attendee types, following the convention `signatureDigest` already set for certification attempts: derived from something a person entered rather than a random token, with identity coming from the session the server already checked. Previously: Records attendance for a class registration with check-in/out times and evidence. Distinct from the class-attendance path already wired in G6; this one is the registration-level record. |
| G15.12 | `approve_training_session_completion` has no caller | S | done | **Wired together with G15.11, because the server ties them together:** approval refuses unless every registration marked `attended` carries signed evidence, and recording that evidence is the only thing that produces it. Wiring either alone would have shipped a button that always failed. The card also disables approval outside `scheduled`/`in_progress`, which the server refuses outright. Previously: Approves a training session's completion with a reason. Nothing offers the approval, so the state it grants cannot be reached. |
| G15.13 | `record_resident_service_task` is a superseded duplicate write path — tracked as SG-4 | S | done | **Folded into SG-4, which is now the only live row for this RPC.** Checked four times from this table, and the fourth check retracted the third: the reason recorded here for not making this a delegating shim — that `completed_by_other` has no equivalent response in the successor, so a shim would lose the fact — was false. `lib/serviceDeliveryContract.ts`'s `completionResponseForServiceOutcome` maps it onto `completed_as_planned` and `useResidentServiceTasks` preserves it in `exception_details` as `legacy_status` plus a boolean, so the mapping a shim needs already exists and is tested. What survives is narrower — the `acceptable_completion_responses` gate a delegating shim would inherit, plus the alerting and validation divergences — and all of it is a behavioural change for callers outside this repository, which is what makes it a standing gap with an owner and a review date rather than a permanently `open` row in a sweep table. `main` opened SG-4 for the same RPC via #452 while this row was in review, reaching the same conclusion — do not write the shim — through the premise this row retracted; SG-4 now carries both, with the premise corrected there and on the function comment (`20260805030000`). Nothing about the RPC changed: still granted, still superseded, still not to be dropped. Previously: a second full implementation writing the same rows on the older status/note/supervisor_notified model, so wiring it would have been actively wrong. |
| G15.14 | `retract_clinical_progress_note` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** Sign and amend were both already on the note row; this third one was not, so a note charted against the wrong resident -- the mistake it exists for -- simply stood. It is not a delete: the server writes the current body into `clinical_progress_note_versions` and sets the note to `entered_in_error`, which is how a clinical record is corrected without losing what it used to say. The dialog says that, and says to amend instead when the point is to change what a note says rather than to withdraw it. Previously: A clinical progress note cannot be retracted. A note entered on the wrong resident stands, because retraction is the audited path and it has no caller. |
| G15.15 | `save_report_schedule` is a superseded shim, not a gap | S | done | **Checked before building, and the finding was real but my diagnosis was wrong.** I recorded this as "a report can be built and run but never scheduled". Scheduling works: the Value Center calls `save_report_schedule_configuration`, which `20260717021844` introduced as a strict superset -- delivery hour and minute, day of week, day of month, and a schedule id so an existing schedule can be edited -- and the same migration re-declared this older signature as a wrapper delegating with defaults of 07:00, Monday, the 1st. So it is dormant by design, and wiring it would have been the wrong answer: the richer function is the one a person should reach. Kept rather than dropped, because it is granted to `authenticated` and an external integration could still call the older signature. The allowlist reason is corrected to say this |
| G15.16 | `set_hris_import_row_decision` has no caller | S | done | **Wired, and it was the missing middle of a flow this branch had already half-fixed.** The Validate card's own description says validation "surfaces duplicate candidates for a human decision" -- and there was nowhere to make one, so a run could be validated and then applied with nothing decided in between, and `apply_hris_import_batch` had nothing to apply. `HrisRowDecisions` sits literally between the two commands. Only rows the server will accept a decision for get a control: it refuses anything whose `validation_status` is not `valid`, and refuses a `link` whose employee is not among that row's own candidates, so the link option is disabled when no candidates were found and the picker offers only those candidates. Offering an action that can only fail is the mistake the funnel dropdown made in G13. Previously: Accepts or rejects one row of an HRIS import against an employee. The import runs are creatable (G10) but individual row decisions cannot be recorded. |
| G15.17 | `get_regulatory_rule_snapshot` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** "What did this rule say on the day this happened" is the question every retrospective compliance argument turns on, and no column can answer it once a rule is superseded. A date with no version in effect is shown as an answer rather than an error. Previously: Returns a rule as it stood on a date. Nothing asks, so 'what did the rule say when this happened' has no answer in the product. |
| G15.18 | `record_regulatory_shadow_run` is a machine entry point | S | done | **Not a gap.** It takes a rule version, a baseline version, a cohort window, an evaluated count and an engine version -- output from a rule engine running a candidate alongside the current one, not anything a person types. The differences it produces ARE now actionable (G15.19). What is missing is the engine that calls it, which does not exist in this repository; giving it a form would be inventing a user for it. Previously: Records a shadow evaluation of a candidate rule version against a cohort -- the mechanism for testing a regulation change before it binds. |
| G15.19 | `reconcile_regulatory_shadow_difference` has no caller | S | done | **Wired.** The resolution vocabulary is read out of the CHECK constraint rather than invented -- a first guess was three plausible words the server rejects. It is `expected_change` / `baseline_defect` / `candidate_defect`, with a rationale of at least ten characters and a 64-hex evidence checksum, both also constraints. Previously: Resolves one difference a shadow run found, with a rationale and an evidence checksum. No shadow run can be recorded, so no difference can exist to reconcile. |
| G15.20 | `record_regulatory_fixture_result` is a machine entry point | S | done | **Not a gap.** One golden-fixture result for a given engine version with a request id: conformance-harness output. Same as G15.18 -- the missing piece is the harness, not a button. Previously: Records a regulatory engine fixture result for a given engine version. The conformance harness has no entry point. |
| G16 | The dormant-RPC gate cannot see an unreachable caller | L | done | **A new class, and the branch that added the dormant-RPC gate proved it by introducing one.** That gate asks whether a database function has a caller. Satisfying it turns out not to mean the capability is reachable: a hook that calls the RPC, that no component renders, is a capability with no way in -- and the gate passes, because the RPC does have a caller. That caller is simply unreachable itself. `useAddAppointmentPreparationItem` was written on this branch, its screen was not, and the gate reported zero; it now has an inline form on the appointments tab. `scripts/check-unrendered-hooks.mjs` closes the class: an exported `use*` hook must be referenced by a `.tsx`, directly or through the *body* of another hook that is itself reachable, computed as a closure because a chain of hooks is only as reachable as the component at the end of it. **Thirty-five hooks had no renderer.** Fifteen were not debt and are deleted -- three unadopted helpers with no caller anywhere (`useIsMobile`, `useServerListState`, `useBulkAction`), nine superseded by a rendered sibling doing the same job better, and three that were never unreachable at all, where the gate itself was reading badly: one declared beside the component using it, and two vouched for by nothing but a comment that had outlived the call it described. Self-tests cover both, since blanking comments and string literals is what makes body-scoped reachability trustworthy. **Thirty-three hooks had no renderer, and all thirty-three are resolved: the allowlist is empty.** Sixteen were not debt and are deleted -- three unadopted helpers with no caller anywhere, and thirteen whose job a rendered sibling already did or whose stated purpose was served another way. Seventeen were real gaps, every one predating this branch, and every one now has a surface. Four of those started life recorded here as gaps and turned out not to be, once the successor was actually read (G16.1, G16.5, G16.18, G16.19) -- the same lesson as G15.13 and G15.15, learned four more times. The gate found its own fourth reading bug by being used (G16.23), which in turn found `queue_clinical_observation_writeback` (G16.24), a whole FHIR write-back pipeline whose queue nothing could fill |
| G16.1 | `useIssueCertificate` is a superseded compatibility endpoint | S | done | **Not a gap, and the migration that superseded it says so outright.** `20260711154819_atomic_course_completion_certificates.sql` moved issuance inside `complete_course_assignment()` because the two-call browser flow left a valid completion with no certificate whenever a request failed between the calls, and records that `issue_certificate` "remains as an idempotent compatibility endpoint". Wiring it would mint a certificate with no completion behind it -- the exact invariant that migration exists to hold. The hook is deleted; the function stays granted for callers outside this repository and is recorded in the dormant-RPC allowlist. **Deleting the hook is what exposed G16.23**, because it left the function with no caller except a string |
| G16.2 | `useCancelTimeOffRequest` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired.** On My Schedule's own time-off card, beside the swap withdrawal G7 added -- both RPCs are scoped to the requester (`employees.profile_id = auth.uid()`), refuse anything not `pending`, and demand a reason of at least five characters, so the control appears only on a pending request and enforces the same minimum. Previously: A submitted time-off request could be approved or denied by a supervisor, but the person who filed it could not withdraw it |
| G16.3 | `useAcknowledgeSupportPlan` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired.** "I have read this plan" on the active version, in the support-plan section of the resident record. Deliberately not behind `canManage`: the server accepts an acknowledgement from anyone whose scope includes the plan, because the record it produces is *this caregiver has read the plan they are working to*, and a manager-only control would capture the wrong people. The server's `on conflict do nothing` treats a double-tap as a double-tap, so once recorded the button becomes "You have read this version" rather than firing again. Previously: the acknowledgement that someone has actually seen the plan could not be recorded, which is the part an inspector asks for |
| G16.4 | `useSupportPlanAcknowledgments` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired with G16.3, because either half alone is worth nothing.** The expanded active version now lists who has read it and when, with any note. Acknowledgements are keyed by plan rather than by resident, so a new active version leaves everyone unacknowledged again -- which is the point of keeping them, and is visible here rather than implied. Previously: nothing wrote an acknowledgement and nothing displayed one, so the table was unreachable in both directions |
| G16.5 | `useLogResidentChangeOfCondition` is a superseded predecessor | S | done | **Not a gap, and my own row said otherwise until I read the successor.** I recorded this as "nothing can put a resident into the change-of-condition queue". Logging a change works: `LogChangeOfConditionDialog` calls `create_resident_change_event` from ResidentDetail and the State Forms Center, and that function writes the identical `significant_change_reassessment` item -- same due date, same warning and grace days -- plus the citation topic, the change event itself, its follow-up and its history, and optionally an incident. The hook is deleted; the function stays, because `convert_shift_report_entry` calls it server-side to turn a triaged handoff entry into the same item. Third time checking before building changed the answer (G15.13, G15.15) |
| G16.6 | `usePinSurveyDayBinder` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired, and reading the trigger changed what it is for.** Pinning is normally automatic: activation pins the latest completed single-facility binder, and `autopin_survey_day_binder` re-pins any binder that completes during an active session. That last part is exactly why a manual pin matters -- a regeneration started by anyone silently replaces the binder a surveyor is being walked through, and there was no way back to the earlier one. The control offers only what the function will accept: succeeded exports whose `facility_ids` is exactly this facility, minus the one already pinned |
| G16.7 | `useRegisterForTrainingSession` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired, and this is the hook that named the class.** G15.11/G15.12 put signed attendance and completion approval onto a roster that nothing could populate. The control is disabled outside `scheduled`/`in_progress`, which the function refuses outright |
| G16.8 | `useSetIncidentQapiConsideration` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired, and this is the most legible form the class takes.** The close-loop checklist has always listed "QAPI consideration" as a stage that blocks closure, and `stageAction` returned nothing for it -- the checklist named the step, marked it outstanding, and offered no button, so an incident that needed the decision could never leave the stage. | G16.9 | `useComplaintTrends` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired.** The Complaints tiles measure the present -- how many are open now, how many are high risk now. What they cannot answer is whether the same complaint keeps coming back, which is what `get_complaint_trends` counts: totals, a category breakdown, and both escalation paths (ombudsman referral, linked incident) over a window. It takes one facility and refuses anything outside scope, so the card appears with a specific facility selected rather than offering a control that would always fail on "All facilities" |
| G16.10 | `useResidentServiceUtilization` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired onto the support-plan tab, which is the point of it.** The plan says what care should be; `get_resident_service_utilization` says what it actually was -- unscheduled services, documented assistance and exceptions over 30, 60 or 90 days. Putting the two on the same tab is the whole value |
| G16.11 | `useFeatureReleaseActive` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired on the release-flags page itself, deliberately not as a gate on some screen.** Cohort membership became administrable in G12.1/G15.1 and nothing read the result, so being in a cohort changed nothing anyone could see. Using it to gate an arbitrary feature would have invented a product decision nobody asked for; what the page was actually missing is confirmation that a change took effect. Each flag row now shows what the whole rollout/cohort/kill-switch evaluation returns for the person looking at it, so flipping a kill switch and watching the evaluated answer turn Off is the difference between having set a flag and having killed a feature. The read fails closed |
| G16.12 | `useUpdateViolation` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired as a partial gap, which is what reading the page first showed.** `status` is deliberately *not* editable here: it moves only through `PocLifecycleActions`' SECURITY DEFINER RPCs, which version the plan of correction and record who verified it, so a raw table update would step around all of that. Previously: a violation could be created, listed, opened and have a POC generated from it, and never corrected, so a typo in a citation stood |
| G16.13 | `useUpdateSchedule` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired, draft-only.** Title and period are editable while the schedule is a draft and not after: the period bounds the assignment grid, so moving it under a published schedule would silently orphan shifts employees have already been shown. Previously: a schedule created by mistake could not be removed |
| G16.15 | `useUpdateCourseVersion` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired, drafts only.** A published version is the record of what people were actually taught; editing it would rewrite history rather than correct a draft, so the control appears beside Publish and disappears with it. Previously: a version could be created, cloned, published and unpublished and its own title and description could not be corrected, so fixing a typo meant cloning the version. `useCompetencies.ts` names this hook in a comment as the pattern to follow, which is exactly how it looked reachable until the gate stopped counting comments as callers |
| G16.16 | `useUpdateEmployeeSchedulePreference` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired.** A typical-pattern row can now be amended in place instead of deleted and rebuilt. This one matters more than it looks: schedule auto-fill reads these patterns, so a stale one is not untidy, it quietly fills the wrong cells |
| G16.17 | `useListAdmissionActivities` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired directly under the form that writes them.** Every call, tour and follow-up was recorded by a rendered control and displayed nowhere, so the trail an admissions conversation depends on -- what was already tried, and how it went -- existed only in the database |
| G16.18 | `useListAllIncidentNotifications` is a dead whole-table read | S | done | **Not a gap; deleted.** It existed for deep-link resolution, and deep links carry the parent id directly -- `/app/incidents/<incident_id>` -- so there was never anything to resolve. | G16.19 | `useListAllIncidentNotificationsDetailed` is a dead whole-table read | S | done | **Not a gap; deleted.** Its own comment said Reports.tsx's incident-notification register used it. The comment described an intention, and the gate believed it until it stopped counting comments as callers |
| G16.20 | `useListAllInspectionEvents` is a dead whole-table read | S | done | **Not a gap; deleted.** Same deep-link resolution as G16.18, gone for the same reason. `useListViolationsBySourceInspectionEvents` is the join that is actually rendered, from the violation side |
| G16.21 | `useListCampaignQuestions` has no renderer | S | done | **Wired into the campaign's own expanded panel.** A campaign is created atomically with its questions by `create_policy_campaign_with_questions`, and after that nothing could show an administrator what it asks -- a campaign already in flight was a black box to the person who owns it. This is the authoring view, so it shows the answer key, behind the same policy-administrator boundary the questions were authored behind. The employee-side read (`usePolicyKnowledgeCheck`, deliberately without the key) was rendered all along |
| G16.23 | The dormant-RPC gate counted a quoted string as a caller | M | done | **Fourth time this script has read SQL wrongly, and this one was found by using it.** Deleting `useIssueCertificate` (G16.1) should have made `issue_certificate` dormant; the gate still passed. Its only apparent call site anywhere was its own error message -- `raise exception 'certificates are not directly writable by clients; use issue_certificate() / service role'` -- and the check counted that string as a call. `blankSqlStrings` now blanks single-quoted literals before the scan, preserving length so offsets hold and treating `''` as an escaped quote rather than a terminator; dollar-quoted bodies are untouched, because that is where real calls live. Then the fix bit immediately in the other direction: the comment explaining *why* the hook had been deleted named the RPC, and the client scan counted prose as a caller too, so `blankTsNonCode` blanks TypeScript comments -- comments only, deliberately not string literals, because in TypeScript every real call goes through `supabase.rpc("name")` and the name IS the literal. Getting that backwards would report the entire schema as dormant. Self-tests 18 -> 25, covering both directions in both languages |
| G16.24 | `queue_clinical_observation_writeback` has no caller | S | done | **Found by G16.23's fix, and it is the substantial one.** The `fhir-writeback` edge function drains a queue that nothing could fill: an observation could be charted and never sent to the resident's EHR, so the entire write-back pipeline had no entry point. It looked reached only because the edge function names it in a comment. Now "Send to EHR" on each observation in the clinical chart. The server refuses a retracted observation, and refuses unless the resident has an active mapping to a write-back-enabled FHIR source -- that refusal names which of the two is missing, which is better than anything guessable client-side, so it is surfaced verbatim rather than hidden behind a pre-emptive disable |
| G16.25 | Hook reachability stopped one layer short | M | done | **The gate had the same blind spot it was built to close, one layer out, and a 209-line panel proved it.** `ReadinessForecastPanel` renders a 30/60/90-day workforce forecast and is the only caller of `get_workforce_readiness_forecast` and `route_workforce_readiness_remediation` -- and nothing imported the panel. Both gates passed: the RPCs had hooks, the hooks had a `.tsx`, and the capability was three layers deep in nothing. "Referenced by some `.tsx`" was never the right rule; "referenced from a module the entry point can reach" is. Reachability is now computed from the real import graph, seeded at `main.tsx` and following static imports and `lazy(() => import(...))` alike, with specifiers read from the RAW source -- an import path IS a string literal, so the string-blanking that fixed G16.23 would have erased every edge, while comments are still stripped so a commented-out import cannot keep a dead file alive. A module nothing consumes is now reported in its own right, and deliberately seeded differently: modules count test files and the entry alike, because a pure helper exercised only by its own unit test is tested rather than dead (`lib/bulkActions.ts` is exactly that), while hooks are seeded at the entry alone, because a component only a test imports still has no way in for a user. Merging the two seeds would have reopened the hole. Self-tests 14 -> 20. The forecast panel now sits above the workforce tab's current numbers, where blockers that have not happened yet belong |
| G16.26 | The hook gate could not see the `const` declaration form | S | done | **Found by auditing the other gates for the failure Codex found in two of them, rather than waiting for a third review to find it.** `HOOK_DECLARATION` matched only `export function useX`. Twenty hooks are declared `export const useX = rpcMutation(...)` -- the whole of `useDietaryOperations` and `useResidentFinancialOperations` -- and none was ever examined. The check then reported the total it had read as though it were the total that exists: "908 exported hooks ... 0 awaiting a surface" was true of the 908 it could see and silent about the 20 it could not. **Every one of the twenty is reachable, so this closes no defect** -- worth stating plainly rather than implying the sweep found something, because the useful fact is that the blind spot was real and the space behind it was empty. The count is now 928 and means what it says. A `const` hook needs a different body boundary: its first `{` is usually a parameter's type literal, so brace-matching from there captures the annotation and stops, and a call in the real body would not propagate reachability. The other gates were audited for the same shape and have none -- `check-dormant-rpcs` and `check-rpc-call-signatures` match on word boundaries and `.rpc(`, which are receiver- and form-agnostic, and the route gate reads quoted literals whatever wraps them, so the SQL `concat('/app/residents/', ...)` form was already covered. Self-tests: 20 -> 27 |
| G17 | A link the server hands a user has to go somewhere | M | done | **The same class as G16, but the caller is the database.** The database and the edge functions build in-app links as string literals -- notification `link` columns, system-job `operator_route` values, and `search_workspace`, which branches on role to send a platform admin to `/admin/<kind>/<id>` instead of `/app/<kind>/<id>`. Nothing verified those paths exist, and nothing could have: the link is assembled in SQL and consumed as an opaque string, and `GlobalSearch` navigates `item.route` verbatim -- no `safePathForRole`, no fallback -- so there was nothing to soften a bad one. Three were broken. `check-server-route-links.mjs` compares every in-app path literal in a migration or edge function against the routes App.tsx declares, treating `:param` as a wildcard and a trailing `/` as the concatenation prefix it actually is. Two parser corrections were needed before the findings were trustworthy: a query string is a deep-link filter and not part of the route (seven notification links carry one), and edge-function TESTS hold fixture URLs that are deliberately not real routes. Self-tests: 13 |
| G17.1 | `search_workspace` sent platform admins to two routes that were never built | S | done | **Wired.** The RPC emits eleven `/admin/...` prefixes for `platform_admin`; nine had a route and `/admin/complaints` and `/admin/violations` did not, so searching a complaint number or a citation reference produced a result that landed on Not Found. The fix is the two missing route declarations, not a redirect: both `/app` equivalents are gated to `org_admin`/`facility_manager`/`auditor` and `platform_admin` is in neither set, so pointing them there would bounce. They reuse `ComplaintDetail` and `ViolationDetail` under `PLATFORM_ADMIN`, which is exactly what the nine working siblings already do, and they are registered in `APP_PAGES` so `routeRegistration.test.ts` holds them to the same parity as every other route |
| G17.2 | The integration webhook dispatch job's Open button went nowhere | S | done | **Fixed by migration.** System Jobs renders `operator_route` as an Open button, and this job's was `/admin/integrations`, which has never been a declared route -- so an operator investigating a stalled webhook dispatch landed on Not Found. It now points at `/admin/enterprise`, where the integration register, credential rotation and webhook endpoint controls actually live (G15.2-G15.5). The original literal stays in the 2026-07-11 migration because history is append-only, so it is recorded in the allowlist naming the migration that supersedes it -- which is what that file is for |
| G17.3 | The route check exempted exactly the links that interpolate | S | done | `serverLinkLiterals` skipped any literal containing `${...}`, on the reasoning that "the prefix is what matters". The effect was that an edge function building a link with a template literal -- the ordinary way a TypeScript function builds one -- was exempt from the check whose entire subject is server-emitted links, while the SQL concatenation form was checked. **The check was reading the case that had already been fixed and skipping the case that had not.** An interpolated value fills one path segment, which is exactly what a `:param` route accepts, so it now substitutes a stand-in segment and checks the result. That surfaced `compliance-copilot` emitting `/app/inspection-items/${row.id}` at two call sites against a route that does not exist; the rows come from `inspection_items` and `/app/inspections/:id` already renders `InspectionItemDetail`, so the link was wrong rather than the routes. One finding, no false positives across 660 server files. Self-tests: 16 |
| G18 | 131 RPC call sites had their type checking cast away | M | done | **Nothing was broken, nothing was protecting it, and the first reason recorded here for leaving it alone was wrong.** `rpc("name" as never, { ... } as never)` appears at 131 distinct call sites, and every one of those names is present in the generated `database.types.ts` -- the casts are not load-bearing, they are left over from code written before the types were regenerated. Inside `as never` a misspelt function or parameter is invisible to typecheck and to every unit test, and surfaces as a PostgREST 404 the first time a real user presses the button. **Removing them wholesale is still the wrong move, but not for the reason first given.** That reason was "155 of the 171 surfaced errors are an explicit `null` against a parameter whose default is also null, so they are semantically identical to omitting" -- generalised from a single sample. Measured properly, the 190 explicit-null arguments split **116 against `default null`** and **74 against parameters with no default at all**, and zero against a non-null default. The 74 break that argument: passing null to a required parameter is not "identical to omitting", since omitting it would be an error -- and they cannot be fixed by editing the call, because **the Supabase type generator does not model parameter nullability**. `create_complaint` declares `p_complainant_name text`, legally nullable (an anonymous complaint has no name), and the generator types it `p_complainant_name: string`. So removing the casts leaves 74 *correct* calls that cannot typecheck at all. That is the real reason to keep them, and it is a limitation of the generator rather than a cost-benefit judgement. `check-rpc-call-signatures.mjs` takes the value without the churn: it reads the migrations for what each function declares and holds every call site to it, cast or not -- the function exists in `public` after every create/drop in filename order; every `p_*` argument is one it declares; no argument is an explicit null where the default is not null; and no argument is an explicit `undefined` where the parameter has no default. Clean on all four across 809 functions and 804 source files, each proved non-vacuous by injecting a fault. Self-tests: 21 |
| G18.1 | The non-null-default rule is the P1 from this branch's own review, made permanent | S | done | PostgreSQL applies a default only when the argument is OMITTED; passing `null` passes null. `start_certification_attempt(p_observed_at timestamptz default now())` was called with an explicit null against a NOT NULL column, so starting a certification attempt failed outright -- a P1 in review, fixed earlier on this branch. The sweep confirms it was the only instance across the 143 functions that have a non-null-default parameter, and the rule now holds that line. Explicit null against `default null` is deliberately **not** flagged: it is identical to omitting the argument, and flagging it is what turned the wholesale cast removal into 155 pointless edits |
| G18.2 | The fourth rule: an explicit `undefined` is an omission, not a value | S | done | supabase-js drops undefined keys from the request body, and PostgREST resolves an RPC by the set of keys it receives -- so an explicit `undefined` against a parameter with no default means the function cannot be resolved at all (PGRST202), not that it runs with a null. Zero instances today across the 570 functions that have a required parameter; the rule holds that line. **What it deliberately does not claim to catch:** a value merely *typed* as possibly-undefined -- `payload.first_name` off a `Partial<>` -- reads as an ordinary expression to a text scanner. Removing the casts surfaces thirteen of those through typecheck, and every one checked is guarded (`enabled:` on the query, or callers that always pass the field), so they are latent rather than live. Catching them needs type information this check does not have, and the gate's comment says so rather than implying coverage it lacks |
| G19 | Sixteen cache invalidations refreshed nothing | M | done | **A mutation succeeds, the toast says it worked, and the list the user is looking at keeps showing the old value.** TanStack Query matches an invalidation by comparing key ELEMENTS, so `["training_class"]` does not match `["training_classes", id]` and `["daily-operations"]` does not match `["daily-operations-command-center", id]` -- string prefixes are not key prefixes. Nothing catches this: it is not a type error, the key is a perfectly good string, and a test that mocks the client sees a call that happened. Seventeen were dead -- sixteen found by the first version of this check, and a seventeenth it could not see, recorded in G19.2. **Six were one keystroke from correct and are now fixed** -- `revoke_class_checkin_tokens` (its ONLY invalidation, so revoking a check-in code left the class page showing it as live), `submit_credential_renewal` -> `employee_credentials`, `determine_incident_reportability` -> `incident_notifications`, and three `daily-operations` -> `daily-operations-command-center`. **Eleven named a concept nothing queries** and are deleted; each sits beside invalidations that do cover the surface, which is why nobody noticed. `check-query-invalidations.mjs` holds the line. Two parser corrections were needed first, and both mattered: key constants must resolve MODULE-FIRST, because several hook modules each declare their own `const QUERY_KEY` with a different root and a flat map silently resolves one module's constant to another's key; and query roots must be collected from sources with invalidations stripped, so an invalidation cannot vouch for itself. Self-tests: 9 |
| G19.2 | The seventeenth was in the check's own header comment, and the check could not see it | S | done | `check-query-invalidations.mjs` opened by listing four bugs "real when this was written". Three were fixed. The fourth -- offline unscheduled-service sync invalidating `["daily-operations"]` -- was still in the tree, and the gate reported a clean pass over it every run, because `INVALIDATE_PATTERN` only matched `queryKey: [` and this key was held in a variable: a per-kind record of key arrays walked in a loop, so no root ever appeared at a `queryKey:` site to be read. **A check that only reads the shape it was written against reports the absence of that shape as the absence of the bug.** `keyRoots` now returns how many keys it could not follow, and a file with an unfollowable invalidation has its array-of-arrays key tables read as the invalidation list -- which is what such a table is for. Caught the surviving bug on the first run, no allowlist entries, no false positives across 265 query roots. Self-tests: 16 |
| G19.3 | Five more invalidations that named nothing, or nothing enough | M | done | Not wrong keys -- missing ones, which the gate cannot see by construction: it asks whether an invalidation matches a query, never whether a mutation invalidated enough. `approve_training_session_completion` inserts a `training_class_attendees` row per attended registration and refreshed neither the roster nor the counts, though every other attendee mutation refreshes both and it writes more rows than any of them. Certification attempts left `["duty-eligibility"]` stale directly beneath a comment reading "a passed attempt grants an employee_qualifications row, which is what duty eligibility reads" -- the comment describes the line that was never written. Citation governance refreshed `["dhs-citation-topics"]` while Violations, ViolationDetail, InspectionReadiness and the assessment surfaces read `["dhs_citation_topics"]`: two hooks over one table under two roots, so verifying a citation updated the governance admin page and left every screen a surveyor looks at showing the old reference. `validate_hris_import_run` left `["hris-import-rows"]` stale, hiding the merge decisions it had just produced from the list rendered directly below its own button. Issuing a survey-packet guest grant did not refresh the list of who holds access, though revoking one did |
| G20 | Break-glass could not be granted at all, because the form was both parties | S | done | **The one path that has to work at 3am failed on its own precondition.** `grant_identity_break_glass` raises 'break-glass requests require a separate approver' when `p_requested_by = auth.uid()`; `BreakGlassCard` filled the requester in with the signed-in admin, so every grant submitted through the UI was rejected. The card was written on this branch specifically because emergency access could not be granted -- and shipped still unable to grant it. **Nothing on the branch could have caught this**: the hook takes `requestedBy` as its own argument and passes it through correctly, the call typechecks, the RPC exists and is granted, and `check-rpc-call-signatures` confirms every parameter is declared. The defect is entirely in which value the card chose, which is a semantic constraint stated in the function body and nowhere in its signature. Requester is now its own field, validated client-side so the refusal arrives while it can still be corrected rather than as a failed submission |
| G20.1 | A card that reported "active" for people with no account | S | done | `EmployeeAccessCard` mounted for every employee and called `is_employee_access_active`, whose `(e.profile_id is null or p.is_active)` clause returns true for an employee with no linked profile. That clause is right for what the function weighs -- a missing profile row is not a suspension -- and wrong as an answer to "can this person sign in", which is what the card's own description promises. The page header two hundred lines above already said "No portal access" for exactly these employees, so the page contradicted itself down the same column. Fixed in the card rather than the function: `is_employee_access_active` is shared, and changing what it returns to suit one screen's phrasing is a behaviour change to everything that reads it |
| G20.2 | A minimum the server did not agree with, and a correction that could not reach the content | S | done | `ReleaseCohortMembershipCard` enforced one `MIN_REASON = 5` across both its actions, but only unassign has a length check and it requires 8 -- so a 5-to-7 character removal reason passed the form and was refused by the server. The two constants are now named for the two rules, with the asymmetry recorded, because it is the SQL that is inconsistent and the client cannot fix that by averaging. Separately, `EmergencyBlockCorrection` could edit only the title, while `admin_emergency_update_course_block` accepts `p_body`, `p_video_url` and `p_document_id`: the wrong dose in the component's own doc comment lives in `body.content`, a field the form could not reach, so the surface could not perform the example it was written for. The body is merged rather than replaced -- `heygen` job state sits beside `content` in the same jsonb and a bare `{ content }` would drop it |
| G20.3 | The third instance of the break-glass class, and the comment that hid it | S | done | **Swept every site where an RPC compares a parameter to `auth.uid()` -- there are three, in two functions.** `grant_duty_eligibility_override` raises 'An override cannot be granted to yourself' when `auth.uid() = p_profile_id`, and `DutyEligibilityCard`'s `canOverride` checked the role half of that constraint and not the self half, so an org admin opening their own employee record was offered a form that could only fail. Much milder than G20 -- one record rather than every submission -- and recorded because of *why* it survived: the prop's own comment already asserted both halves ("The server refuses anyone but an org admin (and refuses self-grants). This only keeps the form from offering what the server would reject"), so anyone reading the call site saw a claim that the check was complete. **A comment describing the intended behaviour is the easiest possible place for a missing half to hide**, which is the same thing that happened in G19.3's certification invalidation, where the comment named the line that was never written |
| G21 | A parameter no caller passes is a capability with no way in | M | done | **The `EmergencyBlockCorrection` finding generalises, and the gate to find the rest was already built.** `check-rpc-call-signatures` parses what each function declares and what every call site sends, so "which declared parameters does no caller ever pass" is a join over data already in hand. **27 called functions have one.** Most are genuinely optional refinements -- an idempotency key, a filter, a link to a related row -- and are not debt. Two are a surface that cannot reach something the record depends on (G21.1, G21.2). The rest are surveyed rather than swept: an unpassed parameter is evidence of a gap, not proof of one, and turning all 27 into work would confuse the two. Deliberately not made a gate for that reason -- it would need an allowlist entry for every legitimately-optional parameter, which is most of them |
| G21.1 | A retracted vital sign left no trace of having existed | M | done | **The chart offers the retraction and then cannot show its result.** `ResidentClinicalChart` has a Retract button that toasts "Observation retracted (entered in error)", and `get_resident_clinical_observations` filters `entered_in_error` rows out unless `p_include_retracted` is passed -- which nothing passed. So the observation, and the `clinical_observation_amendments` row recording who withdrew it and why, became unreachable the moment the button was pressed. "Was this blood pressure ever recorded, and who took it back" is what a clinical record is for, and this is the same shape as G12.5's `employee_access_suspensions` gap -- a state change that leaves no answerable trace on any screen. Retracted rows stay excluded by default, because the chart is what care is delivered from and an entered-in-error vital does not belong in it; a Show retracted toggle renders them struck through, badged, and carrying the reason. Neither Retract nor Send to EHR is offered on one: the server refuses a second amendment with 'Observation is already retracted', and sending a withdrawn vital to the resident's EHR is the opposite of what retracting it meant |
| G21.2 | A care plan goal could never be marked achieved | S | done | `save_care_plan_goal` branches on `p_goal_id` -- insert when absent, update when present -- and nothing ever passed it.
