The plan rendered each goal's status (proposed, active, achieved, on hold, cancelled) and no goal could leave the value it was created with, so a care plan's goals only ever accumulated. The dialog now revises as well as adds. Worth separating from the unbuilt-feature class it resembles: the UI was honestly labelled "Add goal" throughout and promised no edit, so the defect is not a broken affordance -- it is a **status rendered as though it moves, by a product with no way to move it** |
| G21.3 | Status values nothing can set: swept, and clean | S | done | **The G21.2 shape generalised: 48 status-like columns with an enumerated CHECK, 457 distinct values, asking which the client can never choose.** 16 columns have a value the client never names, and on inspection every one is legitimately server- or externally-set and rendered generically through `human()` -- job pipeline states (`generating`, `in_flight`, `retry_scheduled`), notification delivery outcomes (`bounced`, `unsubscribed`), and the external eMAR's `administration_status` values (`late`, `not_available`), which arrive from the integration rather than from charting. **The first two versions of the probe were both wrong and both wrong in the direction of overclaiming**: counting a literal anywhere in the tree found zero, and counting only quoted client strings falsely accused `code_status` -- DNR, DNR-DNI, full code -- of being unsettable, when `CODE_STATUS_LABELS` holds them as unquoted object keys and `EditResidentCareProfileDialog` offers all of them. Recorded because a sweep that reports a clean result is only worth as much as the probe behind it, and this one needed two corrections before its answer meant anything. Noted in passing: `governed_content_review_comments` has `grant select` and no writer at all, so review comments on a governed revision cannot be created -- left alone rather than built, because nothing in the product offers or implies them, which makes it an unbuilt feature and not a broken one |
| G22 | A forced sign-out that left the impersonation tokens behind | S | done | **`MaintenanceGate`'s own comment already names this: "a raw `supabase.auth.signOut()` would strand the impersonation session in sessionStorage."** Three call sites did exactly that. The sharpest is `auth.tsx`'s definitive-profile-absence path, which signed the user out and cleared the query cache but called neither `clearImpersonationSession()` nor `clearSupabaseRuntimeCache()` -- so the ORIGIN platform_admin's access and refresh tokens stayed in `sessionStorage`, `useImpersonationStatus` kept reporting `isImpersonating`, and `MainLayout`'s banner kept offering an "Exit impersonation" button whose handler calls `setSession(originSession)` without checking that the impersonated session is still live. Whoever used that browser tab next had a working route back into a platform_admin session. The deactivated-profile path cleared the impersonation record but not the runtime cache; `MfaSettings`' assurance-refresh failure cleared neither. All four now route through one exported `clearLocalSessionState()`, which is the part that keeps the next forced-sign-out path from picking a subset again |
| G22.1 | The clinical chart's access log recorded the other surface's reason | S | done | `get_resident_clinical_chart`'s `p_minimum_necessary_reason` does not change the payload -- it is the HIPAA minimum-necessary annotation that `log_clinical_access` writes, one row per CALL. `useResidentClinicalChartSummary` keyed on `[key, residentId]` and dropped it, while the two callers pass different reasons: `ResidentClinicalChart` sends "Resident clinical chart view" and `MyResidentChart` sends "Caregiver clinical charting". They shared one cache entry, so opening the second surface within the 60s `staleTime` served the first one's payload, the RPC never ran, and that access produced no log row at all -- leaving an audit trail with one entry carrying the wrong reason. The reason is in the key now; the mutations invalidate on the 2-element prefix, so they still match |
| G22.2 | One undecryptable draft made every other draft unreadable and unsyncable | M | done | `readAllServiceDrafts` and `readAllObservationDrafts` decrypted under `Promise.all`, so a single throwing record rejected the whole call -- and all three callers propagate it: the review list went to an error state, syncing ONE draft failed because a DIFFERENT draft would not decrypt, and sync-all threw before attempting any draft. `decryptDraft` throws on a scope mismatch, on any AES-GCM failure, and on `assertFloorDraftAllowed` rejecting the payload, and that last one is reachable on an ordinary app upgrade: a release that tightens the field guards turns drafts already on the device into throwing records. Meanwhile the plaintext purge clock kept running, so care documentation that was only unreadable was deleted at `UNSYNCED_PURGE_AFTER_MS` without ever having been syncable. Now read per-record and skipped with a logged draft id; the plaintext lane the counts and purge already use still lists it |
| G22.3 | One `CLOSED` set, two different status vocabularies | S | done | `medicationSafetyAnalytics` used `{closed, resolved, completed}` for both `incidents.status` (`reported`/`investigating`/`closed`) and `corrective_actions.status` (`open`/`in_progress`/`completed`/`overdue`/`cancelled`). It covered the incident side and missed `cancelled` on the other, so a called-off corrective action past its due date counted toward `overdueFollowUps` and raised a retraining recommendation off an incident whose follow-up had been deliberately stopped. The SQL computing the same number for the operations snapshot has always excluded both terminal states. `resolved` was in the set and is not legal in either table. Split into two sets that match the CHECK constraints |
| G22.4 | `not_applicable` was in COMPLETED, and the evidence rule made that unreachable | S | done | `moveInReadiness` puts `not_applicable` in `COMPLETED` on purpose, then required a linked signed state form on top of it -- and an inapplicable item is precisely the one that will never have a form attached. It satisfied COMPLETED, failed the evidence test, fell past OPEN and landed on `needs_review`. All three rows using the default `requiresEvidence` are `blocker: true`, so the packet carried a blocker no action could clear and could never reach `inspection_ready` |
| G22.5 | A facility filter applied after the server's LIMIT | S | done | `usePromotableBinderExports` asked for the 25 most recent succeeded exports across the whole organization and only then narrowed to the facility in JavaScript. In a multi-facility org, 25 newer exports belonging to other facilities pushed this facility's out of the window and the page reported no promotable artifacts while they existed. `facility_ids` is `uuid[]`, so containment moves server-side ahead of the limit along with the `content_sha256` predicate; the one-element check stays client-side but now only discards multi-facility exports that already contain this facility |
| G22.6 | Two more invalidations that named nothing, in the shape G19.3 predicted | S | done | `credential-renewal-queue-summary` is a separate root, not a suffix of `credential-renewal-submissions`, so neither `useReviewCredentialRenewal` nor `useCreateCredentialRenewalSubmission` refreshed it. The RPC counts exactly what a decision changes and the query holds `staleTime: 30_000`, so a reviewer approved or rejected an item and watched the queue tiles keep the pre-decision counts. Invisible to `check:query-invalidations` by construction -- the gate asks whether an invalidation matches a query, never whether a mutation is missing one. Also: `/health` was the only server response with no cache directive at all |
| G22.7 | Three destructive-write bugs of one shape: a form that never tracked the record it edits | M | done | **All three saved defaults back over a real record, and two of them regenerate downstream work from it.** `QapiProjectDetail` had no hydration at all -- zero `useEffect` in the file -- so every field sat at its initial literal no matter which project was open, and `update_qapi_project_plan` is a straight `update ... set` with `nullif(btrim(...),'')`, not a coalesce-to-existing. Pressing "Save plan" wrote status back to `active` (regressing a `monitoring` or `pending_closure` project and losing the closure gate it had reached), reset the method to five_whys and the frequency to monthly, blanked `team_members` to `[]`, and NULLed the root-cause analysis, planned interventions, audit sample, barriers, adjustments, effectiveness determination and sustainment period -- the narrative body of the regulatory QI record. `ServiceDelivery`'s `RequirementDialog` is mounted unconditionally and gates itself on `open={!!requirement}`, so its `useState(requirement?.x ?? default)` initializers ran once, on the first render, while `selectedRequirement` was still null: "Configure" showed daily / 09:00-11:00 / DCS / two-staff off regardless of the requirement, and Save wrote that over the resident's service requirement and regenerated the future task instances from it, exactly as its success toast says. `DietaryOperations`' `ResidentNutrition` hydrates on `[profile]` but bails on `if (!profile) return`, and `resident_dietary_profiles` is read with `maybeSingle()` -- so `null` is a resident with no profile, not a pending load, and the form kept the PREVIOUS resident's diet order, food allergies, and texture and liquid consistency to be saved onto the newly selected one. Allergies and consistency are where the wrong resident's values stop being a data-entry annoyance. Fixed by hydrating the QAPI form (on `p?.id`, not `p`, so an action-item invalidation cannot wipe unsaved edits) and by keying the two dialogs on the id they edit |
| G22.8 | A hospital transfer recorded against a resident nobody chose | S | done | `ResidentCareDelivery` computed `residentId \|\| residents.data?.[0]?.id`, and that value feeds only three write actions -- of which the appointment and hospital-transfer cards render no resident picker at all. A user who had not chosen anyone could file a transfer episode or a provider appointment against whoever sorted first, with nothing on screen naming them. The fallback is gone and both cards now carry the same picker the DME card has. The same three mutations passed only `onSuccess`, and `queryClient` sets no global `onError`, so a server rejection produced no toast and no visible change -- indistinguishable from a click that did not register |
| G22.9 | The offline retry loop that could never reach its own deadline | M | done | `useRunAllOfflineSyncs` returned a fresh `run` on every render, and `OfflineSyncManager`'s retry loop lives in a `useEffect` whose deps transitively include it. The effect therefore tore down and re-armed its `setTimeout` from zero on every render, so while the caregiver kept navigating -- or any shell query settled -- the 30s backoff tick never reached its deadline and the backlog was never retried. That defeats exactly what the manager's own header comment says the timer is for: the failures that produce a draft (captive portal, dead route, Supabase down) leave `navigator.onLine` true, so the `online` event never fires and the timer is the only signal left. `run` is now a stable `useCallback` reading the latest body through a ref, rather than pinning correctness to the render-identity of two mutation objects and the toast handle |
| G22.10 | A cleared field that set a quiz's passing score to 0% | S | done | `Number("")` is `0`, and `0` is finite -- so `Number.isFinite(passingScore) ? passingScore : quiz.passing_score_percent` caught `"abc"` but waved an EMPTY field through as a 0% passing score, which `quizzes_passing_score_check` (`between 0 and 100`) accepts without complaint. Clearing the field to retype it and hitting Save made every subsequent attempt a pass on a quiz whose result backs a training certification. Blank now means "leave it alone"; a present value must be in range and is rejected loudly rather than coerced. `max_attempts` had the same `Number("abc")` -> NaN -> null hole and is guarded with it |
| G22.11 | Three more mounted-once dialogs, one of them deleting appended QAPI reasoning | S | done | The G22.7 shape again, found by sweeping for it. `InvestigationStepDialog` seeds its textareas at mount and is rendered unconditionally, while `record_incident_qapi_decision` APPENDS to the very column it edits -- `coalesce(investigation_findings \|\| E'\n\n', '') \|\| 'QAPI not indicated: ' ...` (20260726080100). A manager who recorded a QAPI decision and then opened "Investigation record" saw the pre-append narrative, and saving wrote it back over the appended reasoning. Re-keyed on open so it seeds from the incident as it is now. `CorrectiveActionForm` used `assigneeEmployeeId` as both the guard and a dependency of its default-to-self effect, so picking "Unassigned" -- the one choice its comment promises the reporter -- re-ran the effect and re-assigned the action to them; applied once via a ref now. `CreateWorkItemDialog` stamped a facility-local date with a literal `Z`, making "due today" 19:59 local in a Pennsylvania summer, so from ~8pm `manualWorkItemIssues` rejected it as "in the past" on a day with four hours left; it uses `facilityDayBounds().through` |
| G23 | Every destructive write ran before the only authorization check | S | done | **`accept-learning-package` authenticated the caller and then never asked whether they could touch THIS package.** Having a valid session is not permission. Everything from the package load to the hash update uses the service-role client, which bypasses RLS: it read the row, downloaded the zip, rewrote it, overwrote the stored object at its original path, and updated `content_sha256` -- and the only authorization in the function was `accept_learning_package`'s own check, in the RPC call that came AFTER all of it. So any authenticated user, of any tenant and any role, holding a `learning_packages.id` could have another organization's package rewritten and re-hashed; the 400 the RPC handed back did not undo it. RLS cannot stand in here -- the sole SELECT policy (`learner_packages_select`) requires `validation_status='accepted'` and this function acts on pending/validating/rejected rows, so a caller-scoped read returns nothing for every legitimate call. The rule is restated from the RPC it precedes and evaluated with the caller's JWT before anything destructive runs, answering 404 rather than 403 so a caller who may not act on a package does not learn the id is real |
| G23.1 | A signed download that would package another tenant's compliance binder | S | done | `package-survey-evidence-packet` takes `binder_export_job_id` straight from the request body and looked it up with the service-role client and no `organization_id` predicate. A user entitled to build a packet for their own organization could name another tenant's binder job and receive that organization's compliance binder PDF, appendix manifest and CSVs inside their signed download. `orgId` was already resolved a few lines above -- the binder now has to belong to it |
| G23.2 | The one browser-invoked function using the cron-only CORS style | S | done | `generate-certificate-pdf` built `withCronCorsHeader({"Access-Control-Allow-Origin": "*"})`, and stripping that header is precisely what `withCronCorsHeader` is for ("cron/webhook endpoints are not browser-invoked and must not advertise wildcard (or any) CORS origin"). The responses -- preflight included -- carried no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` at all, so the browser blocked the "Prepare PDF" call `useCertificates.ts` makes and `config.toml` documents this function as serving. Every other `withCronCorsHeader` caller genuinely is cron-only; `generate-compliance-binder`, which `config.toml` names as having this same dual-purpose split, already used the origin-aware helper. Now matches it, with the cron secret named in Allow-Headers so the worker is unaffected |
| G23.3 | The shared kiosk was the one signed-in surface with no idle lock | S | done | `SessionSecurityGates` computes `isKiosk` from the path, reads `kiosk_idle_timeout_minutes` (default 5, against 30 for an ordinary session) and locks with its own `kiosk_timeout` reason -- but `IdleSessionLock` only ever mounted inside `MainLayout`, and the one route carrying `chrome="kiosk"` (`/trainer/classes/:id/kiosk`) renders `KioskLayout` instead. The entire kiosk branch, its setting and its lock reason could never execute, and the shared class-kiosk device -- the one most likely to be left unattended in a room full of people -- had no idle lock at all. Mounted in `KioskLayout` too; `lock()` already returns early without a user, so it degrades to a passive wrapper on any unauthenticated kiosk page |
| G23.4 | Exiting impersonation destroyed the only way back | S | done | On the restore step, `setSession` failing removed the sessionStorage record -- the sole copy of the origin admin tokens. The operator was left authenticated as the TARGET user with no route back and, because the amber banner renders off that same record, no remaining indication the session was an impersonation at all. The record is kept on failure now, so "Exit impersonation" stays retryable and the banner stays up. (Distinct from the `verifyOtp` path in `useStartImpersonation`, which correctly drops a PROVISIONAL record for a swap that never happened.) |
| G23.5 | The agreement you must accept could not be read | S | done | `/legal/facility-signup` -- the platform agreement and BAA -- is a real route linked `target="_blank"` from the consent checkbox on `/signup`, which is itself a signed-out page. It was absent from `isPublicPath`, so the new tab bounced straight to `/login` and the document being accepted was unreachable. Added to `MARKETING_EXTRA_PATHS` alongside `/privacy` and `/terms`. Also `sanitizePostLoginPath` rejected a leading `//` but not `/\`: the URL parser reads a backslash in the authority position as a forward slash, so `?next=/\evil.example` normalized to a protocol-relative cross-origin URL, which wouter's `history.pushState` refuses with a SecurityError -- breaking sign-in for anyone following such a link, and one consumer's `location.href` away from being an open redirect |
| G24 | A worker whose own defaults were infinite recursion | S | done | **`sync-billing-quantities` has never completed a run in production.** Its optional dependency defaults were `getEnv = (name) => getEnv(name)`, `randomUUID = () => randomUUID()` and `nowMs = () => nowMs()` -- a destructuring default is evaluated in the scope of the binding it initializes, so each called ITSELF. `index.ts` injects only `createClient`/`stripePost`/`stripeGet`, so every real invocation took all three and died with `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded` at the first `getEnv("SUPABASE_URL")`, immediately after the cron check. It survived because no test reached the defaults: the unconfigured cases return 503 first, and the one test that goes further injects all three. Replaced with the same real implementations every sibling handler uses, plus a test that deliberately omits `randomUUID`/`nowMs` -- verified to fail with that exact RangeError against the old code. A repo-wide sweep for the same `x = () => x()` shape found no other instance. **2026-08-14: necessary but not sufficient.** The recursion fix was real and is unchanged, but the job still has zero rows in `app_private.system_job_runs` -- it now dies one line later, at the config check, because `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` is not set on the project (A2). Two lessons this row could not have taught on its own, both closed in G269: a 503 that returns before `claim_system_job_execution` leaves no record at all, so "never completed a run" and "never scheduled" look identical on /admin/system-jobs; and the watchdog was reading pg_cron's exit status, which reports that the request was enqueued, so it called the job fresh the entire time |
| G24.1 | Three importers that stamped the UTC day onto a Pennsylvania record | S | done | `_shared/paDay.ts` exists precisely for this and names the four functions it was applied to; three more were missed. `bulk-import-credentials` compared `expiration_date` against the UTC day, so after 20:00 ET a credential expiring today imported as already `expired`. `bulk-import-residents` and the durable `process-data-import-jobs` worker both defaulted a missing `admission_date` to the UTC day, recording an evening admission as tomorrow's -- the date a resident's whole compliance timeline is computed from. All three use `paToday()` now |
| G24.2 | The one importer that reads the whole roster, unpaginated | S | done | `bulk-import-training-records` loads every employee with an `employee_number` into a lookup map, and an unpaginated select is silently cut off at PostgREST's max-rows (1000). Past that the missing employees came back as `Unknown employee_number: X` for people plainly on the roster -- the import refusing rows it should accept, with nothing indicating the roster read was what got truncated. Now paged until a short page. The other importers resolve one entity per row with `.limit(1).maybeSingle()` and were never exposed |
| G25 | A recurring manual campaign opened a cycle with nobody on it | S | done | `spawn_due_policy_campaign_cycles` carries the targeting columns across and copies the knowledge check forward -- explicitly, so "an annual re-attestation" does not "silently degrade to read-and-sign in its second year" -- then called `materialize_policy_campaign_targets`, which returns 0 untouched for any campaign whose `targeting_mode` is not `declarative`. For a MANUAL recurring campaign the worker therefore created the child cycle, copied its questions, enrolled ZERO employees, advanced `next_occurrence_on` and counted itself as spawned. Nothing raised and nothing was flagged; the annual re-attestation simply did not happen, and the only evidence was a campaign that looks open with no participants -- indistinguishable from one nobody has finished setting up. The roster is now carried forward the same way the questions are, re-checked against `employees.status` so a terminated person is not re-enrolled. `20260805120000`, covered by `policy_campaign_cycle_population.test.sql`, which was verified to fail on the old function |
| G25.1 | Two guard rails that raised a Postgres internal error instead of their own message | S | done | **`text[] \|\| 'literal'` does not append in PL/pgSQL.** With an untyped literal Postgres resolves the operator to `anyarray \|\| anyarray`, tries to read the string as an array literal and raises `malformed array literal`. `public.complete_appointment_follow_up` and `app_private.certification_attempt_blockers` both build their refusal message that way, so the moment either had something to report -- a follow-up closed before the outcome was recorded, an attempt blocked by an unpublished checklist version, self-assessment, or an unqualified assessor -- the specific, actionable refusal it was written to give was replaced by an internal error. Both fail closed, so no bad data got through; what was lost is the explanation. Verified against the local stack rather than inferred, and fixed by casting the literal. **`supabase db lint --level error` had been reporting both**, and `pnpm run check:database` runs it with `--fail-on error` -- so the database gate was red on main for this pair, and is green again. `20260805130000` |
| G25.2 | Refuted: declarative enrolment does NOT leave a NULL due date | S | done | Recorded because the code reads exactly like it does, and this was reported as a defect before being checked against a running database. `materialize_policy_campaign_targets` inserts into `policy_attestations` without naming `due_date`, and the column is nullable with no default -- which looks like every declaratively enrolled attestation gets a deadline that `p.due_date < public.pa_today()` and the pending index both lose against. It does not: the BEFORE INSERT trigger from `20260716221235_remediate_policy_attestation_security.sql` assigns `new.due_date := v_campaign_due_date` from the campaign unconditionally, along with the organization, facility, version and status. The trigger owns those columns. `policy_campaign_cycle_population.test.sql` pins that guarantee to the overdue predicate that depends on it, so removing the assignment fails a test rather than quietly disabling every overdue count |
| G26 | The gate certifying 34 unauthenticated edge functions was satisfied by an import | S | done | **The same vacuity this check has now had twice.** Its own comment records the first: the marker matched `export function requireCronRequest` inside `_shared/cronAuth.ts`, so any function importing that module for anything at all was certified as cron-gated, and `blankDeclarationHeads` was added to stop it. But an IMPORT is not a use either, and `import { requireCronRequest } from "../_shared/cronAuth.ts"` still matched -- so a function could have its gate call deleted and keep passing. That certification is what stands behind 34 functions running with `verify_jwt = false`. Measured against the old and new module side by side: import-only proved the gate `true` before and `false` after, while a real call still proves it. Import statements are now blanked whole (newlines preserved, and `importClosure` walks the raw text so the closure is unchanged), with three self-test cases covering both directions -- 15 now, and the real tree still certifies all 73 functions, so nothing was relying on an import to look gated |
| G26.1 | A fork PR could deploy unmerged code to the production database | S | done | `deploy-migrations.yml` triggers on `workflow_run` for the CI workflow with `branches: [main]`, and that filter matches the triggering run's HEAD BRANCH NAME. CI runs on `pull_request`, including from forks -- so a fork PR opened from a branch the contributor happens to have named `main` satisfies it. The job's only other condition was `conclusion == 'success'`, which the fork's CI supplies for the fork's own code, and the checkout takes `github.event.workflow_run.head_sha`: the fork's commit. That is a path from opening a pull request to running `supabase db push` and deploying edge functions against production on unmerged, unreviewed code, with the `production` environment's reviewers as the only thing in the way. The trigger now additionally requires `workflow_run.event == 'push'`, `head_branch == 'main'` and `head_repository.full_name == github.repository`; a fork PR is always `pull_request` from another repository |
| G26.2 | Two more gates that failed open | S | done | `deploy-migrations.yml`'s path short-circuit ran its `git diff` under `\|\| true`, making an ERRORED diff indistinguishable from one that found nothing -- and "nothing" means skip the deploy. An unfetchable previous SHA (force-push, pruned object, shallow fetch) therefore skipped a production deploy and reported success, which is the exact failure the workflow's own header says it exists to close ("migrations merged to main could sit undeployed indefinitely"). The status is captured now and an undetermined diff deploys rather than skips, which is the safe direction because `supabase db push` skips migrations already applied. Separately, `check-dormant-rpcs.mjs` excluded `*.test.ts` from the CLIENT walk but not the EDGE walk, so an RPC whose only caller was a Deno edge-function test counted as reached -- a function called only by its own test is what dormant means. Both walks exclude tests now; the check still passes, so nothing was relying on it |
| G27 | Two crosswalk rows that could never return to covered | S | done | `evaluateEvidence`'s `physical_site` branch carries an explicit guard -- "Completed/cancelled corrective actions keep their past due_date forever; they must not register as permanent physical-site gaps via the overdue check" -- and the `resident` and `policy` branches, which have exactly the same problem, did not. A resident compliance item completed even one day LATE is `compliant` with `due_date < today` from then on, and `policy_attestations.status` is only pending/attested with signing leaving `due_date` untouched. Both therefore counted as open gaps forever, so an obligation whose evidence was fully satisfied still reported uncovered and no action available to the facility could clear it. That is worse than a miscount: the number never moves. Both branches now exclude the satisfied statuses from the date-based check, covered by two tests verified to fail against the old code |
| G28 | Two CI jobs budgeted for less than their own clone | S | done | `planning-registers` and `changes` both spend essentially their whole runtime on a `fetch-depth: 0` clone -- the checks themselves finish in under a second -- and both were capped at `timeout-minutes: 5`. On `016401c` that checkout took 4m59s and the timeout cancelled the job mid-checkout, which failed the aggregate `ci-result` and so the PR, for a reason with no connection to the code; the retry cloned the same commit in 25s. That is the shape worth naming: not a flake, a budget one slow clone away from failing on any PR, and when it fires it looks like the register check found something. `changes` is the worse of the two because every other job is gated on its outputs, so a slow clone there takes the whole run rather than one check. Both raised to 10, matching `migration-immutability` -- the closest analogue, full history plus a fast script -- and still below `secret-scan` (15) and `application` (25) |
| G29 | Evening incidents, a literal backslash-n, three 404 links, and a search that answered to the forbidden term | S | done | Sweep of the remaining lib-layer findings. `incidentAnalytics.daysSince` ended its window at `T23:59:59Z`, which is 19:59:59 in Pennsylvania -- an incident reported at 20:30 was AHEAD of "the end of today", so `daysSince` returned -1 and both `days >= 0` recency filters dropped it: the last-7 and last-30-day counts understated exactly the freshest, most likely still unresolved incidents. `calendarExport` joined its description lines on the two-character sequence `\n` and then ran `escapeIcsText`, which escaped the backslash again -- calendars showed a literal `\n` instead of a line break. `carebaseGlossary` pointed three terms (Violation, Plan of correction, Attestation) at `/app/compliance`, which `App.tsx` does not register, so the glossary's own "related route" button rendered the 404 page; `check-server-route-links` enforces precisely this rule but only for links built in migrations and edge functions, so a client-side registry is outside its scope -- a sweep of every client `href:` literal against the route table found no others. And facility-type search answered to `ALR` but not `ALF`: `documentTemplates` matched the stored code only, while `dhsFormsLibrary` had bolted on `query.includes("alf")` -- an inverted substring test, so "half" returned every ALF form. Both now use one `facilityTypeMatchesQuery` helper that matches the code OR the displayed label, which also picks up "assisted living" and "personal care". Separately `pchAlrOperationalSnapshot.severityFor` took a `watchThreshold = 1` that all sixteen call sites left at the default, making `"watch"` unreachable and the dashboard's "Attention buckets" metric merely "buckets with anything open"; the dead dial is gone, and choosing a real threshold is left as the product decision it is |
| G30 | A 24-hour grace period nobody granted, a DME no card could see, and a day that depended on where you were sitting | S | done | `residentNeedsAttention` used one overdue predicate -- `daysBetween(...) > 0`, a whole-day floor -- for both a DATE column and a `timestamptz`. For the instant it was simply wrong: a change-of-condition follow-up due at 09:00 did not count as overdue until 09:00 the NEXT day, so every follow-up had a silent 24-hour grace period on the card whose entire job is to say a resident needs looking at now. Split into `isDateOverdue` (facility day) and `isInstantOverdue` (the moment it passes). **Both medical-evaluation cycles were absent from `ASSESSMENT_ITEM_TYPES`, and that set gates BOTH the assessment card and the missing-state-form card** -- so a missing or overdue DME (55 Pa. Code 2600.141 / 2800.141) produced no card from any path. An existing test asserted that exclusion by name; it now asserts the type that is legitimately excluded, `support_plan_30day`, which has its own dedicated cards and would otherwise raise two for one obligation. `residentCareConflicts.localCalendarDay` derived its day from `getFullYear/getMonth/getDate` -- the VIEWER's timezone -- so a return recorded at 21:00 in Pennsylvania resolved to the next day for anyone on a UTC machine and the same record produced different conflicts for different staff; it uses `facilityToday()` now, the same day `pa_today()` computes server-side |
| G30.1 | Refuted: work-item source routing and the two lookback windows | S | done | Both reported, both wrong, both worth recording because the first is easy to re-report from a partial read. `sourceRouteForWorkItem` was said to be missing cases for five registered source types; extracting the full switch shows 34 cases against 32 registered keys -- every one is routed, plus a `default: return null`. The earlier count came from reading a truncated line range, which is the failure mode to avoid on a long switch. And `residentNeedsAttention`'s 7-day service-exception window was reported as disagreeing with change detection's 14-day one: they are different signals. The assistance and refusal thresholds ARE imported from `residentChangeDetection` and shared, exactly as the file's header promises; the 7-day figure pairs with `serviceExceptionsLast7Days`, the RPC fallback aggregate it substitutes for, so the typed and untyped paths agree with each other |
| G30.2 | A bookmark that said "suspend", equipment changes nobody saw, and a reason that was dropped rather than deferred | S | done | `learningRuntime`'s suspend-data chain ended in `raw["cmi.core.exit"]`. That is SCORM's exit MODE -- "suspend", "logout", "time-out" -- not suspend data, so a package that set an exit mode without any suspend data had the learner's bookmark stored as the literal string `"suspend"`, and resuming handed the content that string as its saved state. `cmi.suspend_data` is the real key in both SCORM 1.2 and 2004 and was already in the chain. `hospitalReconciliation.recordedChanges` omitted `dme_changes`, which is in `RETURN_CHANGE_FIELDS`, is asked for by the return dialog, and is written by `complete_hospital_return` -- so a return whose only recorded change was new equipment (a walker the resident did not go in with, oxygen, a lift) displayed "no changes recorded" on the section a nurse reads before revising the plan, which is the change most likely to alter a transfer-assistance service. And `employeeReadiness`'s `conditionally_ready` verdict listed due-soon credentials but not due-soon training; because that verdict RETURNS rather than falling through to `expiring_soon`, the training reason was not deferred to a later branch, it was dropped |
| G30.3 | Refuted: the corrective-action verification blocker | S | done | Reported as counting non-completed actions into an unclearable closure blocker. It does not: the `unverified` branch is inside `if (open.length === 0)`, so it only runs once every live action is complete, and `live` at that point contains exactly those completed actions. The message it produces ("N completed actions have no verification recorded") is accurate, and recording verification notes clears it |
| G31 | Three more gates that reported on something they had not examined | S | done | `check-date-only-parsing` scanned FILE CONTENT line by line, and its balanced-paren walk needs the closing paren to be on the same line. A Prettier-wrapped call -- `new Date(\n  row.effective_date,\n);` -- left the walk with `arg` equal to just `"new Date("`, which contains no column name, so the call site was skipped and the file reported clean. Proven rather than argued: planting exactly that call in `complianceDates.ts` makes the old checker exit 0 and the new one report `src/lib/complianceDates.ts:35`. The walk never cared about newlines; only the caller did. Now scans whole files and derives the line number from the match offset, with the wrapped forms added to the self-test (15 cases). `check-migration-policies` resolved its migrations directory from `process.cwd()` while already computing `SCRIPT_DIR` for its allowlist, and its ENOENT branch printed "No supabase/migrations directory found; nothing to lint" and exited 0 -- so run from anywhere but the repo root (a hook, an editor task, a CI step with `working-directory`) the lint passed without linting. It resolves from the script now and a missing directory throws. And `ci.yml`'s path filter omitted `railway.json`, `railpack.json`, `tsconfig.base.json` and `.nvmrc`: a PR touching only those set `application=false` and `database=false`, every gated job was skipped, and `ci-result` aggregates a skipped job as success -- so it merged with no validation at all |
| G19.1 | The incident one is the sharpest, because the code said what it wanted | S | done | `useIncidentFollowThrough`'s own comment reads: "a single call routinely changes all three -- determining reportability, for instance, also creates or stands down notification rows." It then invalidated `["incident-notifications"]` while the query is `["incident_notifications"]`. The intent was written down, the hyphen defeated it, and the notification list never heard about rows the same call had just created |
| G32 | A forgeable IP on a signed legal record, an unauthenticated buffer, and a preview that approved rows the apply refuses | S | done | Sweep of the remaining edge-function findings, all five on functions running `verify_jwt=false`. `submit-confidential-intake` carried its OWN `clientIp`, taking `cf-connecting-ip` unconditionally and otherwise the FIRST hop of `x-forwarded-for` -- the half of that list the caller writes. `_shared/clientIp.ts` exists because that is backwards (the last hop is the one the gateway observed and appended, and `cf-connecting-ip` is honored only under `CF_FRONTED`), and this is the endpoint where it matters most: the value keys the per-IP rate limit, so a fresh fake per request defeated it, and it is also stored as the ESIGN attribution on the intake -- an attacker-chosen address written onto a signed legal record. `integration-api` checked `content-length` and then called `req.text()`; a chunked request has no such header, `Number(null ?? "0")` is 0, and the whole body was buffered into memory BEFORE the API credential on the next lines is authenticated. `readTextBody` aborts the stream at the cap instead. `survey-packet-guest-download` had the same unbounded `req.json()` and additionally returned the raw Postgres error text from `resolve_survey_packet_guest_token` to a caller who holds nothing but a token; it is logged now and answered with a fixed message. `bulk-import-incidents`' dry run accepted a summary of 8 characters while BOTH apply paths -- `record_incident_from_import` and the durable worker's `apply_incident_import_row` -- reject `length(btrim(narrative)) < 10`, so the preview called rows valid that the import then refused, which is the one thing a preview exists to rule out. And `fhir-writeback` treated a failed `complete_fhir_writeback` as an ordinary error, but the resource is already POSTed by then: the row stayed claimable and the next run re-sent it, duplicating a clinical record in the customer's EHR. Retried three times with backoff, and if it still fails the external resource id is logged at error level -- it is the only handle for reconciling the duplicate and exists nowhere else once the function returns |
| G33 | A SECURITY DEFINER helper that restated one of the three rules its own RLS policy states | S | done | `materialize_policy_campaign_targets` writes `policy_attestations` with the service-role-equivalent authority of SECURITY DEFINER, so `policy_attestations_insert` is never evaluated on the rows it creates and the function has to restate that policy itself. It restated the role/tenant rule and neither of the other two. **`is_assigned_to_facility(facility_id)`**: that predicate is true unconditionally for org_admin and auditor, so it constrains exactly one role -- facility_manager -- and `policy_attestation_campaigns` has no facility column and an org-scoped insert policy, so a facility_manager may author a campaign with `target_facility_ids => null`, which means every facility in the organization. Materializing it enrolled every active employee in the org. The same person inserting the same row through PostgREST is refused. Reproduced against a real Postgres with the helper bodies copied from their migrations: acting as a manager assigned to Facility A only, the old function returned `enrolled=2` and left an attestation row at Facility B; the new one refuses and writes nothing, while the manager's own facility-scoped campaign still enrols. **`identity_assurance_is_current('policy_document_admin')`**: every call arriving through `create_policy_campaign_with_questions` has already passed this, because that RPC is SECURITY INVOKER and its campaign insert is governed by `policy_attestation_campaigns_write` -- but the materializer is granted to `authenticated` and reachable directly over PostgREST with no campaign insert ahead of it, and `policy_document_admin` is in the program-wide baseline `identity_operation_requires_aal2` applies even with no tenant policy row. Both checks sit inside the existing `auth.uid() is not null` branch, so the daily sweep and the recurrence spawner -- which carry no JWT and are gated by the EXECUTE grant -- still reach every matched facility and stay idempotent, verified. Refusing rather than silently narrowing the insert is deliberate: a partial enrolment is indistinguishable from a complete one on the campaign screen, which is the failure G25 and 20260803060000's own note both describe. The target set moved into `app_private.policy_campaign_matched_employees` so the scope check and the insert cannot come to disagree about which employees a campaign matches. `20260805140000`, covered by `policy_campaign_enrolment_scope.test.sql` |
| G34 | Two appliers on one import ledger, and only one of them ever took the claim | S | done | **The durable worker and the browser applied the same import rows to the same customer.** `claim_data_import_jobs` claims any job in `ready` or `applying` whose claim is absent or expired, and `process-data-import-jobs` then applies every `data_import_rows` row still marked `valid`. A browser apply is EXACTLY that shape: the eight `bulk-import-*` functions walk the CSV in chunks and hold the job at `applying` between them, and nothing on that side had ever written `claim_expires_at` -- so an import a manager was halfway through was indistinguishable from one a dead tab had stranded, which is the case the worker was built to rescue. Both then applied the same rows, because the browser's loop reads the CSV rather than the ledger. Not an error on either side: duplicate employees, residents, credentials, contacts, assessments and incidents in a customer's data, from an import that reported success twice. All eight durable domains were exposed. The mechanism to prevent it already existed and only one participant was using it. `record_data_import_chunk` -- the single RPC every browser chunk already goes through -- now holds the job's claim for its caller, renews it per chunk, refuses when someone else holds a live one, and clears it the moment a phase is reported finished. Two things follow with no change to the worker: `claim_data_import_jobs` skips a job a session is working (its `claim_expires_at` predicate finally has something to read, and its `for update skip locked` passes over the row this function locks), and calling `record_data_import_chunk` with an EMPTY chunk and no status is an atomic lease acquisition -- which is what the importers now do before applying their first row, because discovering the conflict when the receipt is written is too late, the duplicates already exist (`_shared/importJobLease.ts`). Measured against the old and new function side by side on a real Postgres: with the old one a worker sweep claimed the job mid-apply with four ledger rows still `valid`; with the new one it claims none, a second session is refused, the holder is not locked out by its own claim, a finished phase hands the job straight back, and an EXPIRED claim is still takeable -- the stranded-run recovery the worker exists for, kept. `20260805150000`, covered by `data_import_apply_lease.test.sql` |
| G35 | Which annual rule-pack row a resident got was the planner's choice | S | done | `resident_compliance_rule_packs` is keyed by (organization_id, state, facility_type, item_type, admission_track) -- its own lookup index says so, and `instantiate_resident_compliance_items` reads it that way, substituting `'standard'` for PCH, which has no expedited track. The successor lookup 20260804170000 added to `complete_resident_compliance_item` dropped two of those predicates: it matched on item_type, facility_type, is_active and tenant, then `order by rp.organization_id nulls last limit 1`. For ALR that matches BOTH seeded `annual_medical_evaluation` rows -- standard and expedited, derived from the two `medical_evaluation` rows 20260706155617 seeded for 2800.141 -- and both are global, so they tie on the only ORDER BY key and LIMIT 1 returns whichever row the plan reached first. The two rows disagree: `warning_days` is carried from the source row, 30 on the standard track and 14 on the expedited one. So an ALF resident's annual medical evaluation began warning 30 days out or 14 days out with nothing in the data to say which, on the cycle whose entire purpose is to raise the item before it lapses. Reproduced on a real Postgres with both seeded rows present: the old function handed an EXPEDITED resident `warning_days=30`, the standard track's window; the new one gives 14 while a standard resident still gets 30. The predicates are restated as the rest of the schema writes them, with the same PCH substitution, and the ORDER BY made total (`created_at desc, id`) so a duplicate key cannot reintroduce a coin flip. `20260805160000`; `medical_evaluation_cycles.test.sql` gains an expedited resident, and its existing assertions are untouched because the resident it already had is on the standard track |
| G36 | The seeded catalog still called it an Assisted Living Residence | S | done | CLAUDE.md's rule is that every customer-facing string says Assisted Living Facility (ALF) while the STORED CODE stays `'ALR'`. The app source had been swept and is clean -- every remaining `ALR` in it is an identifier, a constant name or a comment, and `facilityTypeLabel` has always produced the ALF label. What no sweep had reached is the text seeded into TABLES, which the app renders verbatim: `dhs_citation_topics.title` is the violation topic picker, the governance list and the dashboard's topic badges, `.category` sits under it, `courses.title`/`.category` are the catalog a learner reads, and one onboarding template label is what a new ALF hire is measured against. Those rows said "ALR Medical Evaluations", "ALR Annual Topics", "ALR 18-hour initial training". Three things make it a data change rather than a copy change and each is handled: `dhs_citation_topics.category` is a UNIQUE lookup key that `auto_tag_resident_compliance_item_citation_topic` selects on by literal, so the trigger is redeclared with the literals moved in the same migration -- renaming the rows alone would have left every new ALF resident-compliance item with a NULL citation topic, silently untagged, which is worse than a wrong word; only the PLATFORM catalog is rewritten (`organization_id is null`), because a course a customer named is their copy; and the prefix is matched as `'ALR '` with the trailing space so `catalog_code`, `training_type_code` and `topic_code` (ALR-18HR-INITIAL, ALR-DIRECT-ANNUAL, ALR-2800.65-I1) are untouched -- they are joined on and never shown as prose. The migration ends with a guard that raises if any category the trigger asks for does not exist, verified to pass on the renamed data and to fail on a deliberately stale rename. The courses half additionally needs `app.privileged_write`: `validate_course_catalog_publication` fires BEFORE UPDATE on every published course and re-runs the whole publish gate, whose readiness check refuses a caller who is not a platform admin -- so a pure copy edit failed authorization as `postgres`, which the database CI job caught before this reached main. `20260805170000`; `medical_evaluation_cycles.test.sql` moves with it |
| G37 | A required build-time variable set to a single space passed the check that exists to stop it | S | done | `vite.config.ts` throws when `VITE_SUPABASE_URL`, `VITE_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` or `VITE_TURNSTILE_SITE_KEY` is missing, because Vite inlines them and a bundle built without them is a blank page while `/health` still returns 200 and Railway calls the deploy healthy. The test was `!env[key]`, which catches unset and `""` and not `" "` -- and the file's own optional-variable warning, eight lines below, exists precisely to say that a whitespace-only value is TRUTHY and ships as itself. So the one input the gate did not test produced exactly the deploy it was written to prevent: `createClient(" ", " ")` throwing at module init. A Railway `${{...}}` reference that resolves to a padded value gets there without anyone typing a space. Now `!env[key] || env[key].trim() === ""`, with the message saying which case it is |
| G38 | A pre-departure sign-off that outlived the thing it signed for | S | done | `set_appointment_preparation_item_ready` already states the rule and states it well -- "Reopening an item after the pre-departure sign-off would leave `preparation_completed_at` asserting something that is no longer true" -- and clears the sign-off in the same statement. `add_appointment_preparation_item` is the other way to make it untrue and did not. Its item defaults to `required => true` and inserts `ready = false`, so adding one to an appointment already signed off produces exactly the state the reopen guard prevents; the ON CONFLICT branch reaches it too, by promoting a not-ready optional item to required. Not a display quibble: `complete_appointment_preparation` refuses while anything required is outstanding and cannot be called again on an appointment that is already signed off, so the new item never gets its gate. The nurse who adds "oxygen cylinder" twenty minutes before transport sees a green sign-off on an appointment with an outstanding required item, and the sign-off is the thing meant to say otherwise. Cleared now, conditional on the item actually being required and not ready -- verified on a real Postgres across all four cases: required-and-outstanding clears it, an optional item does not, re-adding an already-ticked item does not, and promoting an outstanding optional item to required does. `20260805180000` |

| G39 | Four submit buttons that stayed enabled after their date field was cleared | S | done | `new Date("").toISOString()` raises `RangeError: Invalid time value`, and raising it out of an onClick is a blank page, not a validation message. Five components already get this right by refusing to submit while the datetime-local field is empty (`!startsAt`, `!returnTime`, `!identifiedAt`, `Number.isNaN(new Date(...).getTime())`); four did not. `ResidentServicesCalendar`'s reschedule and create dialogs gated only on ORDERING -- `ends <= starts` -- and every non-empty string is greater than `""`, so clearing "Starts" left the comparison false and the button live; its follow-up drafts were added on title/description length alone, so a cleared due field reached the same call at submit. `BreakGlassCard` is the sharpest of the four because its guards read as thorough: `expiryPast` and `expiryTooFar` are both `!!expiry && ...`, so an EMPTY field violates neither and `canGrant` stayed true -- the emergency-access grant, of all things. A usable expiry is now its own condition rather than the absence of a violated one, which also covers an unparseable value, where every comparison against NaN is false |
| G40 | Three more places where the day was the browser's, not the facility's | S | done | `IncidentDetail` stored the final report submission date as `new Date("2026-08-05").toISOString()`, and a bare date string parses as UTC MIDNIGHT -- 2026-08-04 20:00 in Pennsylvania. So the date a manager typed was filed as the PREVIOUS day on the one field 55 Pa. Code measures a reporting deadline against, and the two read-backs beside it disagreed with each other about which day it was. `facilityDayBounds().from` resolves the same calendar day the way `pa_today()` does. `AuditLog`'s export manifest built its window from `new Date("YYYY-MM-DDT00:00:00")`, which is the VIEWER's midnight -- an auditor on Pacific time got a manifest starting three hours after the facility day the operator asked for, and the manual `setDate(+1)` on the end was reaching for what `facilityDateRangeBounds().through` already gives. And `ComplaintTrendsCard` defaulted its two pickers from `new Date().toISOString().slice(0, 10)`, the UTC day: from 20:00 ET the card opened on TOMORROW and looked back ninety days from there, against an RPC that reads `date_received::date` in the facility's calendar. `check-date-only-parsing` does not catch any of these -- it matches `new Date(<column name>)`, and all three go through local state variables |
| G41 | A page clamp that fired before the count it clamps against was known | S | done | `Residents`, `Incidents` and `Alerts` each run `if (page > totalPages) setPage(totalPages)` in an effect, where `totalPages` derives from `query.data?.count ?? 0`. On the first render `data` is undefined, so `totalPages` is 1 -- and the effect fires immediately, rewriting a deep-linked, bookmarked or Back-navigated `?page=7` to `?page=1` and refetching the wrong page before the real count ever arrives. Every one of those three pages stores its page in the URL specifically so that Back returns to the same view, which this defeated. The clamp is for a page that no longer exists after a filter narrows the set; "we have not asked yet" is not that, so it now waits for the count to be known |
| G42 | Five links that pointed at the domain root of a deploy that is not served from it | S | done | `vite.config.ts` reads `BASE_PATH` into Vite's `base`, so every route can be prefixed -- and ten link builders reached for `window.location.origin`, five of which prepended that prefix and five of which did not. The five that did not are the ones that leave the app: the invitation reset-password redirect, the class check-in QR code, the training-passport QR, the anonymous safety-report link a facility posts publicly, and Stripe's return/success/cancel URLs. Under a base-path deploy every one is a 404, and a QR code and a payment redirect are exactly the links nobody notices are wrong until someone outside the building follows one. The asymmetry is why it survived: at the domain root, which is how the app is run in development, the broken form and the correct one produce the same string. `lib/appUrl.ts` is the rule now -- `appPath` for in-app routes, `absoluteAppUrl` for links that get pasted or scanned -- and the five sites that were already correct use it too, since ten hand-rolled copies is what let half of them drift |
| G43 | An unconfigured quiz that reported as an error, a maintenance gate that never refetched, and a discharge that failed in silence | S | done | `useGetQuizByBlockId` used `.single()`, so a course block with no quiz yet -- the ordinary state of a block being authored -- came back as a PGRST116 ERROR, retried three times, and was then reinterpreted by the caller as "No quiz configured yet". The failure was being used as the empty signal, which also means a genuine fault (a row RLS filtered out, two rows on one block) read as "not configured yet". `.maybeSingle()` makes null mean absent and an error mean an error. `usePlatformStatus`'s own comment says "refetchOnWindowFocus (default) covers tab re-focus sooner" -- but `lib/queryClient.ts` sets `refetchOnWindowFocus: false` app-wide, so returning to a backgrounded tab did not re-check the maintenance gate at all and waited out the poll interval instead; it opts back in explicitly now, which is the exception that gate earns. And `ResidentDetail`'s status Select was the only mutation on the page with no error path: a refused discharge (RLS, or a trigger holding it until something is documented) left the control showing the new value until the next refetch quietly snapped it back, saying nothing -- and silence reads as success on a status change |
| G44 | A single-facility convenience that deleted the resident it was navigated to | S | done | Three pages (`DietaryOperations`, `ResidentServicesCalendar`, `ResidentFinancialOperations`) auto-pick the facility when an org has exactly one: `if (!facilityId && facilities.length === 1) setFacilityId(...)`. But `useResidentNavigationContext` resolves `facilityId` as `state.facility || linkedResident.data?.facility_id`, and on a deep link of the form `?resident=X` with no facility that second term is undefined until the resident query returns -- so on the first render `facilityId` was empty, the effect fired, and `setFacilityId` cleared `resident`, exactly as a USER changing facility should. The deep-linked resident was gone before the page finished loading, on precisely the single-facility orgs the convenience exists for. Fixed in the hook rather than three times over: `adoptDefaultFacility` seeds the facility without clearing anything and refuses outright once the URL carries either value, because a seeded default has no business overwriting the state the caller arrived with. `setFacilityId` keeps its clearing semantics for a real facility change |
| G45 | An evidentiary date that moved every time the form was saved | S | done | `BackgroundChecks` wrote `non_disqualification_statement_signed_at`, `supervision_attestation_confirmed_at` and `suitability_determined_at` as `flag ? new Date().toISOString() : null` on every save. So re-opening a profile to fix a typo in the notes moved all three to today -- the date the OAPSA non-disqualification statement was signed, the date supervision was confirmed, and the date suitability was determined, which are the dates a surveyor asks for. The record kept saying "the last time anyone touched this form". A flag that was already set now keeps the timestamp it was set with; only a newly-set flag is stamped, and clearing one still clears it |
| G46 | A CSV column that was blank in the view it is usually exported from | S | done | `ComplianceCommandCenter` resolves each row's building name through `useComplianceFacilityBuildings`, which was `enabled: !!facilityId` -- and the page passes `undefined` whenever the facility filter is on "All facilities", its default. So in the most common view of the page the query never ran, `buildingName()` returned `""` for every row, and the CSV a facility hands to a surveyor had an empty Building column: not marked unknown, just blank, which reads as "no building" rather than "we did not look". The hook fetches every building the caller can see when no facility is named (RLS scopes the unfiltered read the same way), the building FILTER still only offers the selected facility's, and an id that genuinely cannot be resolved now says so |
| G47 | Six generic DOM ids on a page that already knew better | S | done | `EvidenceCollectionDetail` computes a `useId()` prefix and used it for exactly one field; the other six carried hardcoded ids -- `guest-label`, `revoke-reason`, `withdraw-reason`, `evidence-export`, `evidence-display-name`, `guest-expiry`. Those are generic enough to collide with anything else mounted at the same time, and a duplicate id sends both the label's click and the screen reader's announcement to whichever element the document reached first. All seven derive from the prefix now |
| G48 | A termination case that showed a UUID where the person's name goes | S | done | `EmployeeLifecycleCases` fetches employees with `status: "active"` and uses that one list for two things with opposite needs: the create dialog's picker, which should only offer people who can still be transitioned, and `employeeName()`, which labels EXISTING cases. A termination case is by definition a case whose employee is no longer active, so the screen that reviews terminations fell through to `id.slice(0, 8)` -- a truncated UUID in the column where a person's name belongs. The fetch is unfiltered now and the picker narrows to active in a memo |
| G49 | The filing gate refused the incidents that need filing most | S | done | `Incidents`' create dialog blocks a major/critical incident with no manually-added notification, and its own message says "(or the auto state-hotline entry)" -- a notification the check did not account for. `auto_create_incident_notifications` files a state-hotline row (and a law-enforcement one for abuse and assault) on insert for EVERY incident type the form offers except `other`, so a critical death or abuse allegation could not be filed until the reporter hand-entered a duplicate of the row the database was about to create. On the pathway where minutes matter, the guard rail was in the doorway. The auto-notified types are now named once in `incidentStages.ts`, mirrored from the trigger's preset table with a test that pins the pair together, and the manual requirement applies only to `other`, which is the one type no obligation can be inferred from |
| G50 | A comparison card that opened on nothing, and a QAPI meeting recorded in the future | S | done | `SupportPlanVersionComparison` seeded its two version selects with `useState(() => ordered[0]?.id)`. A lazy initializer runs once, at mount, and `plans` is `query.data ?? []` -- so it ran against an EMPTY array, stored `""`, and when the versions arrived the card rendered its full comparison UI with both selects blank and no diff. Its entire purpose is to open on latest-versus-previous. Both ids are derived with a fallback now, which also self-heals if the selected version stops existing. Separately `QapiProjectDetail` built two `datetime-local` defaults as `new Date(...).toISOString().slice(0, 16)` -- a UTC wall clock handed to an input that renders it as LOCAL time, so at 21:30 ET the "held at" field opened on 01:30 the NEXT day and submitting it recorded a QAPI meeting as held in the future, on the wrong date; reading back was already local, so the round trip disagreed with itself. `toDateTimeLocal` is what the rest of the app uses. Both submit paths also gained the G39 blank-input guard -- the meeting button did not check `held` at all |
| G51 | A compliance report that stopped at a thousand rows | S | done | `useListAllResidentComplianceItems` ran one unpaginated select, and PostgREST caps those at max-rows (1000 by default) with no error -- just a short list. It backs the resident compliance REPORT and the State Forms Center queue, every resident carries roughly half a dozen items, so a 170-resident organization is already past the cap. Because the rows are ordered by `due_date` ascending, what fell off was the far end of the calendar, and nothing on screen said the list was incomplete. Paged until a short page, the same way `useComplianceInstances` already does it and for the same stated reason -- this is the third instance of the shape after G24.2 |
| G52 | Enterprise SSO could not be completed at all | S | done | `signInWithSSO` does not sign anyone in: it returns the identity provider's authorization URL and the caller has to send the browser there -- the method's own documentation is literally `if (data?.url) window.location.href = data.url`. `Login.tsx`'s mutation returned that data and dropped it. So "Continue with enterprise SSO" resolved SUCCESSFULLY, cleared its pending state, raised no error, and left the user sitting on the login page. The whole enterprise sign-in path was unreachable, and the shape of the failure -- success with nothing happening -- is why it reads as a broken button rather than a bug anyone reports. Redirects now, with an explicit message for the case where a domain has no SSO connection configured |
| G53 | Two dialogs that reopened holding the last person's details, and a shortcut that worked once | S | done | `AdmissionOperations` renders `ProspectDialog` and `RoomDialog` unconditionally, so their field state was created once at mount and never reset: after adding a referral the dialog closed with every value still in it, and opening it for the NEXT person showed the previous one's name, date of birth, phone and contact details, pre-filled and ready to save. Re-keyed on open, the same fix as G22.7/G22.11. Its move-in workspace link also went to `window.location.href = \`/app/...\`` with no base path (G42's shape). And `Employees`' `?action=add` handler ran on a query-string CHANGE but never consumed the parameter, so following "Onboard employee" from the sidebar or the dashboard a second time -- having closed the dialog once -- set an identical search string and opened nothing: the shortcut worked once per visit and then looked broken. The parameter is cleared once acted on, which also stops the URL asserting a dialog that is no longer open. Reviewed on #458: the `?action=` effect was flagged for reading `canManage` and `basePath` without listing them, on the theory that a cold load sees `canManage === false` before auth resolves and never retries. That path is not reachable -- `useAuth` keeps `isLoading` true until the PROFILE query resolves, and `ProtectedRoute` renders `FullPageLoading` until then, so the page cannot mount before `user.role` is settled. Both values are listed anyway (`b4ab814`): the effect's last act removes the `action` param, so an extra run returns at the guard, and the code no longer leans on that `ProtectedRoute` guarantee holding |
| G54 | Refuted: APP_PAGES role metadata does not disagree with the route guards | S | done | Reported as a registry drifting from `ProtectedRoute`, and recorded because the first check I wrote APPEARED to confirm it -- one hit on `/app/invitations`, which turned out to be my own regex pairing a path with a later route's `allowedRoles` because the route between them spelled its roles as an inline array rather than a named constant. Resolving both forms and splitting on route boundaries first: all 134 registered pages compared against 175 routes, zero pages offered to a role the route refuses. The check is worth keeping even though it found nothing -- nothing else stops someone widening `APP_PAGES.roles` without widening the route, and that produces a page the app offers and then refuses, which a user cannot tell apart from a broken permission. Added to `routeRegistration.test.ts` with a vacuity guard so it cannot pass by resolving no routes. The REVERSE direction is deliberately not asserted: a route admitting a role the page does not advertise is how two intentional postures are expressed (trainers are navigated to `/trainer/*` equivalents, platform_admin can open several `/app/*` operator surfaces that no admin menu lists) |
| G55 | Refuted: EmployeeCredentials does not save silently | S | done | Reported alongside the ResidentDetail discharge (G43), and worth recording separately because the two look alike and only one was real. Every mutation on this page carries an `onError` with a destructive toast -- create, update, delete, and document delete -- and the upload and download paths are `mutateAsync` inside try/catch with the same treatment. The `verified_at`/`verified_by_profile_id` re-stamp on save is also not the G45 defect it resembles: the code says what it is doing and why ("who last recorded a verification, and when", with prior values in `audit_logs`), which is a different column contract from BackgroundChecks' one-time attestation dates. Nothing changed |
| G56 | Six review findings on this PR, five of them defects I introduced | S | done | Codex review of #458. **P1, and the worst of them:** `BackgroundChecks` (G45) preserved the attestation `_at` while still writing the current user into `_by`, so the pair now DISAGREED -- an attestation made in March by one administrator was attributed to whoever edited the notes in August. That is worse than the bug it replaced, where actor and timestamp at least agreed. An attestation is one fact, who and when, so it is preserved, stamped, or cleared as a unit; a CHANGED suitability verdict now re-stamps rather than inheriting the old determination's actor. **`CorrectiveActionForm`** (G22.11): the ref that stops "Unassigned" being undone was never cleared, and all three call sites keep the form mounted -- so the first action of a visit defaulted to the filer and every one after it silently did not. `resetForCreate` re-applies the default directly, because the effect's dependencies do not change on a reset and it would never re-run. **`regulatoryCrosswalk`** (G27): excluding satisfied records from the gap count left `summarize`'s `?? sortedDates[0]` fallback reporting the oldest of those settled dates, so a row could read "inspection ready" beside a next-due date that had already gone by; a past date is only meaningful when something is outstanding. **`incidentAnalytics`** (G29): `daysSince` divided elapsed milliseconds by 86_400_000, which is not a calendar-day count -- across Pennsylvania's November fall-back an incident seven facility dates old measures 8, and the windows are inclusive `<= 7`, so it dropped out of the recency counts for about a week after every transition. Reproduced (8 vs 7 on the exact input) and now differences facility DATES read at UTC midnight, which has no zone in it. **`EmployeeLifecycleCases`** (G48): dropping the server-side `status` filter traded a correctness bug for a scale bug -- one unpaginated read capped at 1000 rows, with inactive records consuming the window. `useListEmployeesByIds` already exists for precisely this ("payload proportional to the work on screen"); the picker is server-filtered to active again and case labels are a targeted lookup of the ids on the page. **`offlineServiceDraftCache`** (G22.2): an unreadable draft was logged and omitted, while the header's count comes from the plaintext lane that still lists it -- so the panel read "N pending" above a row that never appeared, "Sync now" attempted nothing and reported success, and nothing cleared it before the purge deadline. My own comment called that "nothing is concealed", which was the wrong reading of the same fact. The ids come back with the drafts now and render as an unreadable row with the one action that still works on them -- dismissal needs no decryption |
| G57 | Second full-app review after #458: auth holes, fail-open gates, stranded publishes, and empty-success errors | L | done | Picked up where #458 left off. **`evaluate_learning_path`** accepted any caller whose `current_org_id` matched the assignment, so an ordinary employee could submit outcomes for a coworker; it also reevaluated completed assignments into contradictory state. Tightened to assignee / privileged roles / active-only (`20260805190000`). **`MfaPolicyGate` and `IdleSessionLock` failed open** while loading and after query errors — both now fail closed with retry/sign-out (enrollment route still reachable). **Policy publish** was two Data API writes; a failure between them stranded an immutable published version that was not current — `publish_policy_document_version` is one transaction. Assessment autosaves are serialized and finalization awaits the latest attempt (including a failed one). Customer PDFs no longer print raw `ALR`. Empty datetime fields no longer throw `RangeError`. End-of-day stamps use Pennsylvania via `facilityDateTimeToUtc`. Several surfaces that treated query errors as empty success now show `QueryError`. Auditors can no longer overwrite POC PDFs. Facility managers can delete compliance evidence they can upload (`20260805191000`). Portal revoke/share/reply dialogs clear state on close so one grant's draft cannot act on another. Follow-on: document delete no longer ignores Storage failures; support-ticket create rolls back the empty ticket when the first message fails; support ticket/message list views page past PostgREST max-rows; conversation query errors no longer look like an empty thread |
| G58 | Third-pass review: auditor packet writes, FM cross-facility grants, orphan regenerates, readiness fail-open | L | done | **Auth.** Auditors could `add`/`remove` survey packet items and `assemble` (audit side-effects) while storage INSERT and product copy treat them as read-only — dropped from those writers; `list` still admits them (`20260806010000`). Facility managers could issue/revoke survey packet guest grants and submit credential renewals for any facility in the org; both now require `is_assigned_to_facility`. Packet item add/remove likewise. **`save_learning_path_version`** took `max(version_number)+1` without a lock; advisory xact lock per definition serializes it. **Edge / storage.** POC regenerate updates the existing `poc` row instead of delete-then-insert. Survey packet packaging removes the ZIP when the export RPC fails and fails hard on signed-URL errors. Settings logo awaits the settings upsert and cleans up a new object key on failure. Support reply attachment cleanup surfaces remove failures like create. **Readiness / lists.** Training/credentials/incidents/corrective-actions/inspection-items page past PostgREST max-rows; InspectionReadiness treats loading and query errors as unknown, not Ready. **Calendar / classes.** Resident services datetime-local values go through `facilityDateTimeLocalToUtcIso`. Draft classes get Open for enrollment (`status=scheduled`) so SessionRoster is reachable. **Fourth pass.** `assert_can_manage_violation` (POC lifecycle) and `record_survey_evidence_packet_export` / packet list RPCs also lacked FM facility scope (`20260806020000`); packaging edge function checks assignment before service-role binder download; SCORM zip upload cleans up on register failure |

| G61 | The drift checker read byte offsets as string indices and called the result deployment drift | S | done | `Deploy migrations` had been red on main since before #458 -- three files reported as CONTENT drift against the deployed ledger. Read the ledger directly (`supabase_migrations.schema_migrations` on the live project, the same Management API endpoint the checker uses) and the three are not one problem but two. **Two are real post-deploy comment edits**: strip `--` comments and collapse whitespace and `20260802080000` and `20260804200000` are byte-identical to their deployed statements (md5 `95701bcb...` / `5571b58d...`), so what runs is unchanged and they are recorded in `migration-content-allowlist.json` with those figures. **The third was never drift at all**, and neither were two of the entries already sitting in the allowlist. `supabaseStatementsJoined` reconstructs the form Supabase records at apply time by slicing the file at libpg-query's `stmt_location`/`stmt_len`. Those are UTF-8 BYTE offsets -- positions in the C string the parser saw -- and `String.prototype.slice` indexes UTF-16 code units. The two agree only while a file is pure ASCII, and diverge by one index for every extra byte before the statement, cumulatively: the three `·` in `20260804110000` shifted its closing `revoke ...;` / `grant ...` pair into `... anon; g` + `rant execute ...`, a difference that exists nowhere in the file. Slicing a `Buffer` by those offsets is the whole fix. It also cleared `20260704210000` and `20260801220000`, whose written allowlist exceptions turn out to have been describing this same artifact rather than any real divergence -- both now match the deployed ledger exactly, so the entries are removed. A `--self-test` fixture with four multi-byte characters ahead of three statements holds the line; under UTF-16 slicing it reassembles as `re`/`voke` and `gr`/`ant`, which is the failure this had been mistaking for drift. Worth keeping in view twice over: a gate that fabricates a failure spends the same credibility as one that misses a real one -- this one had been holding seven migrations out of production while pointing at an innocent file -- and an allowlist is where a fabricated failure goes to look permanent, since every entry is a written argument that the tool was right |
| G62 | Bulk alert resolve/dismiss threw after a successful write, and single resolve skipped audit/`resolved_at` | S | done | `bulk_update_alert_status` returns `{ idempotencyKey, total, …, results: [...] }`. `useBulkUpdateAlerts` cast that envelope to an array and called `.filter`, so every bulk action failed in the client after Postgres had already updated the rows -- optimism rolled back and the toast said failure while the audit trail said success. `parseBulkAlertStatusResult` reads the envelope (with a bare-array fallback). Separately `useUpdateAlert` still wrote `alerts` directly, so one-click resolve/dismiss skipped the RPC's `resolved_at` stamp and `alerts_bulk_status_updated` audit row that bulk already used; both hooks now share one RPC path |

| G269 | A critical job failed hourly for weeks and the watchdog called it healthy | S | done | **`billing-quantity-sync` is registered `is_critical` with a 3-hour freshness SLA and had never once succeeded.** Two independent reasons, both here. **The critical definition had no cron name.** `run_system_job_watchdog()` iterates `where is_active and is_critical and cron_job_name is not null`, and two rows described the one job -- `billing-quantity-sync` (critical, `cron_job_name` null) and `billing-quantity-sync-cron` (carried the cron name, not critical). The split fell exactly across the watchdog's two filters, so neither half was iterated; every other edge-function job carries both on a single row. **And pg_cron "succeeded" is not the job succeeding.** The resolver took `greatest(own_success, cron_success)`, where `cron_success` is the newest `cron.job_run_details` success. For the 13 cron entries whose command is a `net.http_post` to an Edge Function that status records that the *request was enqueued*, not that the function did the work -- so `last_success_at` advanced every hour while the function returned 503 every hour, and the SLA could never be breached. Same failure mode `20260726250000` was written to close, one layer down: there the job was invisible for having no definition row, here because its health signal measured the wrong thing. The critical definition now owns the cron name (the duplicate is removed, guarded by its own run history since the FK is `ON DELETE RESTRICT`), and the watchdog credits the cron-side signal only for `sql_cron`, where the scheduled command *is* the work -- `edge_cron` and `worker` must prove success with a finished `system_job_runs` row. The handler also records the misconfiguration instead of returning 503 into the void. Blast radius is three rows: `notification-dispatch` unchanged (thousands of recent successes), `billing-quantity-sync` stale as intended, and `exclusion-screening` newly stale -- not a regression, a second critical job read off the same wrong sensor, and it needs its own look |
